## 3. What separates strong from loose (the empirical comparison)

I picked ten explanations that were clearly strong and ten that were complete but loose, read all twenty side by side, then measured a few things.

- **Strong (10):** PT139-S4-Q18 Flaw, PT142-S4-Q22 Flaw, PT145-S2-Q19 Paradox, PT154-S1-Q18 NA, PT153-S2-Q13 Weaken, PT146-S2-Q13 Strengthen, PT147-S1-Q17 Evaluate, PT152-S2-Q21 Principle-Conform, PT150-S3-Q15 Agree/Disagree, PT144-S3-Q17 Role.
- **Loose but complete (10), with the failure each shows:** PT127-S2-Q3 Weaken (two wrong answers get one line each: "This supports the argument's explanation"); PT126-S4-Q20 Weaken (generic summary notes, a Goal that restates the task); PT124-S3-Q10 Role (short, and "Haha. This answer is unthinkably weird"); PT151-S2-Q18 Strengthen (117 words total; the 29% trap gets one sentence); PT146-S2-Q8 Flaw (every wrong answer is "The argument never discusses X", correct note is one line); PT142-S4-Q5 Principle (mechanical, no reason why the trap tempts); PT155-S2-Q10 Paradox (fine idea, thin notes); PT158-S3-Q2 Strengthen (long, but misquotes a choice); PT158-S2-Q16 Paradox (long, but brings in outside facts); PT148-S1-Q20 Method (sound analysis, but calls the correct answer "so, so dumb").

Measured averages (strong vs loose):

| Measure | Strong | Loose |
|---|---|---|
| Total words | 773 | 394 |
| Words in the Evaluate step | 169 | 77 |
| Words in the Goal step | 66 | 43 |
| Goal names 3+ content words from the stimulus | 0.9 | 0.5 |
| Avg words per wrong-answer note | 87 | 45 |
| Wrong-answer notes under 20 words (verdict only) | 0.0 per question | 1.1 per question |
| Wrong-answer notes that run an explicit test (negate it, does it weaken, does it trigger the rule, why should we believe it) | 1.4 of 4 | 0.2 of 4 |
| Words in the correct-answer note | 143 | 60 |
| Examples, analogies or worked numbers | 2.0 | 0.5 |
| Stated generalizations ("whenever", "on X questions", "usually") | 1.6 | 0.3 |

What actually separates them, in order of how reliably it predicted my judgment:

1. **The Evaluate step argues with the argument.** Strong ones do real work before the choices: they build the objection ("Given that X, how could we argue not-Y?"), do the arithmetic (25% of $400 vs 13% of $1,000), or set the two populations side by side. Loose ones restate the stimulus and move on.
2. **The Goal names the answer, not the task.** Strong: "look for an answer saying the fish in the leafless trees are exposed to predators that hunt by sight." Loose: "Find the flaw that mistakes a response to danger for a cause", "Identify an answer choice that supports fairness." Strong Goals often give two acceptable shapes (a linking answer or a defender).
3. **Every wrong answer gets a reason tied to this question, and usually a test.** The loose group's signature is the verdict-only note: "This is irrelevant.", "That is unnecessary.", "The argument never discusses profits." Strong notes say what the answer claims, what was needed, and where they diverge, often by running the family's test on it (negate it; would it weaken; is the trigger established; does the conclusion match the rule's right side).
4. **The popular trap gets the most attention.** Strong explanations spend their longest wrong-answer note on the choice that drew 15-30% of students and say why it tempts. Loose ones treat it like every other wrong answer.
5. **The correct-answer note explains the fit, and often generalizes it.** Strong: "Another correct answer performing the same function could have said..." (PT146-S2-Q13); the karaoke/Jenga reason why A → C still works when you predicted A and B → C (PT139-S4-Q21). Loose: "This is the flaw."
6. **Fidelity.** Two loose items are long. Length did not save them: a misquoted answer choice, imported outside facts, or the author disowning the question destroys model value regardless of word count.

What did not separate them: word count by itself (PT152-S4-Q19 is 479 words and strong; PT158-S2-Q16 is 716 and loose), the choice-tag vocabulary, and the presence of jokes (several strong ones joke; it only hurts when the joke is at the question's or the answer's expense).

## 4. Rubric used for the shortlist

Gate (hard requirement): full Argument breakdown and all five choice notes. Every shortlisted candidate passed.

Scored 0 / 1 / 2:

| Criterion | 2 | 1 | 0 |
|---|---|---|---|
| **P** Prediction | Goal names the content or shape of the right answer before the choices (may give two acceptable shapes) | Goal names the task or admits it cannot predict | No prediction |
| **X** Precision | Names the exact gap, flaw or relationship in this argument's words | Uses the right label but stays generic | Label only, or wrong |
| **E** Eliminations | Every wrong answer gets a question-grounded reason; the popular trap gets the most attention | Most do; one or two verdict-only notes | Verdict-only notes |
| **T** Transfer | States the move in a form that works on the next question of this type (the test, the rule, the recurring trap) | Transfer is implicit | None |
| **C** Clarity | Plain, no padding, no pasted repetition | Some padding or pasted notes | Hard to follow |
| **L** Learner value | Teaches what a student needs to understand and repeat the move: why the trap tempts, what to do when stuck, a worked example or analogy, a second correct answer that would also work | Justifies the key well but does not teach the move | Only justifies the key |

Plus a **fidelity flag** (not scored; disqualifying if serious): `slip` factual or typo error, `tone` the author mocks the question or answer, `register` explicit-Standard PT157/158 prose, `transcription` damaged text.

Learner value is the criterion that most often separated a 10 from a 12. Many explanations justify the key correctly (1) but only the best ones also show the student the move they will reuse (2).

## 6. Recurring phrasing conventions

- **Frames:** "Given that ... how could we (still) argue that ..." (Weaken, Strengthen, NA, Flaw); "Curious Fact ... Author's Explanation ... Alternate Explanation / Plausibility" (any causal argument); "Given that ... How can it be that ..." (Paradox); "If Evidence, then Conclusion" (Principle, SA).
- **Tests in choice notes:** negate it and ask if it weakens (NA); "fails to consider X" means ask whether X weakens, "presumes X" means ask whether X is necessary (Flaw); "argues X by Y: X must match the conclusion and Y the evidence" (Method, Flaw); a rule can only prove its right-hand side (Principle, SA); "can we support the Agree position and the Disagree position?" (Agree/Disagree); "Why should we believe this?" (Role, Main Conclusion).
- **Recurring trap names:** Too Strong (the most common NA and MBT tag), Out of Scope, No Impact, Unclear Impact, No Distinction (Paradox), Deepens Paradox, Wrong Flaw / Not an Objection (Flaw), Bad Conclusion / Evidence / Trigger Match (Principle, Parallel), Premise / Last-Claim Trap / Opposing Position (Main Conclusion), Unsupported Agree/Disagree Position (Agree/Disagree), Fake Opposite / Illegal Negation (NA), Reversal (SA). Tags come from the local markdown; the dump does not include them.
- **Rules stated as rules:** "Most X can only be concluded from a Most X premise"; "some / sometimes / can is almost always wrong on Strengthen, Weaken, Paradox, SA"; "the second speaker almost never denies the first speaker's evidence"; "about 85-90% of Main Conclusion questions put the conclusion in the first sentence or after a pivot"; "on EXCEPT, three of the working answers usually work one way and one works another".
- **Voice habits that should not be copied:** mocking the question or answer ("hot garbage", "so, so dumb", "unthinkably weird", "dumpster fire"), and the PT157/158 em-dash register.

5	STIMULUS: Educator: Environmental factors clearly have little effect on whether a teenager will participate in sports. Family life is probably the strongest environmental factor, yet it is common for one teenager in a family to participate in sports enthusiastically while other teenagers in the family are indifferent to sports. Moreover, school programs designed to encourage inactive teenagers to participate in sports are generally ineffective.
6	STEM: Which one of the following, if true, most seriously weakens the educator’s argument?
7	(A) [29% | No Impact or Strengthens] Athletic ability varies, even among siblings, and teenagers who have demonstrable athletic ability are more likely than those who do not to participate in sports.
8	(B) [11% | Too Weak] Some teenagers, even those in schools that do not have any sports programs, are more enthusiastic about participating in sports than their parents are.
9	(C) [10% | Out of Scope: adults' enthusiasm] Adults’ enthusiasm for participating in sports generally is directly proportional to the extent to which they participated in sports when they were younger.
10	(D) [44% | Correct] The proportion of teenagers who participate in sports varies greatly from society to society and from decade to decade.
11	(E) [6% | No Impact / Strengthens] School programs designed to encourage inactive teenagers to participate in sports widely vary in success, with only a few being highly successful.
12	--- BREAKDOWN
13	## Conclusion
14	Environmental factors clearly have little effect on whether a teenager will participate in sports.
15	## Evidence
16	The strongest environmental factor is probably family life, yet it's common for one teenager in a family to participate in sports while others in the same family don't like sports.
17	Also, school programs designed to get inactive teens to play sports are generally ineffective.
18	## Evaluate
19	We have an Anti-Causal conclusion, so we'll be trying to argue that "environmental factors DO have much effect on whether teens participate in sports".
20	We can weaken the argument by "making excuses" for the author's premises or by introducing new evidence that helps us say "environmental factors do have a lot of effect".
21	Given That
22	Within a family, some teens play sports and others don't
23	How Could We Argue That
24	family life is operating as an environmental factor?
25	We would say a number of things:
26	- who says that the family is trying to influence all its kids in the same direction? (maybe you have an old-school Dad who tries to move his son towards sports but moves his daughters away from sports).
27	- what if the younger siblings want to differentiate themselves from the older sibling that got into sports?
28	Given That
29	School programs that try to get inactive teens into playing sports usually don't work,
30	How Could We Argue That
31	environmental factors do influence whether teens play sports?
32	Maybe we could say that these cheesy assemblies exert influence, just in an opposite, backfiring direction. Maybe teens are being convinced out of getting into high school sports based on who unappealing these school programs are.
33	Or we could just say that these programs are poorly designed and that if properly designed they would be able to exert some environmental influence.
34	## Goal
35	Look for an answer "explaining away" either of the two premises in a way that protects the idea that environmental factors are playing a role, or for any answer that helps us argue that environmental factors are playing a role.
36	--- CHOICE NOTES
37	(A) Athletic ability is not an environmental trait, it's an innate one. Environmental factors can certainly lead someone to augment or diminish their abilities, but this answer is explaining the variation within families in a way that seems closer to Innate factors than Environmental factors.
38	
39	The tall / fast / strong kid is more likely to play sports than the short / slow / weak kid. That's not environmental factors having effect; that's innate physiological factors.
40	(B) The word "some" is wrong 98% of the time you see it in Strengthen, Weaken, and Paradox.
41	
42	Can we really weaken this argument by saying, "Oh, author, did you know that at least one teenager exists who likes sports more than their parents?"
43	
44	It isn't even really worth figuring out whether that data point agrees or disagrees, when it's just one measly data point that can't sway this conversation much.
45	(C) This seems to suggest that environmental factors (did you play sports growing up) can have a big effect on whether an adult will participate in sports.
46	
47	But it's not doing much to assess how that adult got into spots as teenager in the first place (Environmental or Innate factors?)
48	(D) This is a legendarily horrid correct answer. It's in my top 20. It has nothing to do with either Premise, which is why it's easy to dismiss on a first pass.
49	
50	But, it allows us to push back against the author's conclusion. There aren't that many types of factors in the world: Environmental, Innate, and maybe that's it (random factors?)
51	
52	Given that the proportion of teens who plays sports varies greatly across societies and decades, the author (who thinks Environmental factors have little effect) would have to say that Innate factors are the reason?
53	
54	Even if you grant the author the suspect idea that Innate factors vary greatly across societies, nobody's gonna say that Innate factors vary greatly from decade to decade.
55	
56	Since these data points are not compatible with a "Innate factors determine whether teens participate in sports" hypothesis, they suggest instead that Environmental factors must be at play here.
57	(E) We already knew that these school programs are generally ineffective (51% or more of them). But that premise left plenty of room for the idea that some of them are effective, even highly successful.
58	
59	Since this fact is fully absorbed within the negative space of the 2nd premise, it doesn't change the conversation.
60	
61	And since the conclusion isn't totally absolute, just "Environmental factors clearly have little effect", the author is willing to tolerate some tiny exceptions to his broad generalization.
62	
63	To the extent that this answer says, "Hey ... we've tried tons of different approaches, and only a few have worked", it actually beats back one of our anticipated objections (what if your school programs are just lame, and a well-designed one would actually work?)
64	
65	This answer suggests that we've tried a wide spectrum of approaches, and the fact that the vast majority of them failed starts to help the author's case.

68	=================== PT141-S2-Q9 | diff 3 | key C | score 25.2 | features: Explain Curious Fact, Alternatives
69	STIMULUS: Columnist: Research shows significant reductions in the number of people smoking, and especially in the number of first-time smokers in those countries that have imposed stringent restrictions on tobacco advertising. This provides substantial grounds for disputing tobacco companies’ claims that advertising has no significant causal impact on the tendency to smoke.
70	STEM: Which one of the following, if true, most undermines the columnist’s reasoning?
71	(A) [19% | No Impact / Relative vs. Absolute] People who smoke are unlikely to quit merely because they are no longer exposed to tobacco advertising.
72	(B) [1% | No Impact: broadcast vs. print] Broadcast media tend to have stricter restrictions on tobacco advertising than do print media.
73	(C) [75% | Correct] Restrictions on tobacco advertising are imposed only in countries where a negative attitude toward tobacco use is already widespread and increasing.
74	(D) [2% | No Impact] Most people who begin smoking during adolescence continue to smoke throughout their lives.
75	(E) [3% | No Impact] People who are largely unaffected by tobacco advertising tend to be unaffected by other kinds of advertising as well.
76	--- BREAKDOWN
77	## Conclusion (this provides grounds for)
78	Tobacco companies are wrong to claim that advertising has no significant causal impact on the tendency to smoke.
79	*rephrase:* Advertising **does** have a significant causal impact on the tendency to smoke.
80	## Evidence
81	Research shows significant reductions in the number of people smoking, especially the number of first-time smokers, in those countries that have imposed stringent restrictions on tobacco advertising.
82	## Evaluate
83	This argument is presenting a *Correlation* in its evidence:
84	"in countries where X is true, Y is true"
85	And the argument is assuming *Causation* in its conclusion:
86	"X has a significant causal impact on Y"
87	Hence, we could use our *Explain Curious Fact* mindset.
88	*Curious Fact ...* How come countries that impose stringent restrictions on tobacco advertising see significant reductions in the number of people smoking?
89	*Author's Explanation ...* the restrictions on advertising cause the reduction in smoking, because advertising has a significant causal impact on the tendency to smoke.
90	These types of Explain Curious Fact arguments always have the same two pressure points:
91	- Alternate Explanations for the curious fact
92	- the plausibility of the Author's Explanation
93	## Goal
94	On Weaken questions, it's more common for them to weaken a causal conclusion by suggesting an Alternate Explanation for the curious fact.
95	So let's look for an answer that gives us a different way to explain why the countries that imposed restrictions on tobacco advertising saw significant reductions in smoking (maybe there's some 3rd factor that explains both the laws against tobacco advertising and the reduction in smoking).
96	But we'll keep our mind open to any answer that undermines the plausibility of the idea that "ads for cigarettes have a significant causal impact on the tendency to smoke".
97	--- CHOICE NOTES
98	(A) When we first read this, it somewhat sounds like it's undermining the idea that "tobacco ads have significant causal impact on the tendency to smoke".
99	
100	After all, it's saying that people are unlikely to quit merely because they no longer see tobacco ads. But we're not concerned about whether they are likely / unlikely (absolute) to quit smoking. We're concerned with whether they are **more** likely to quit smoking than they were when the ads were still allowed.
101	
102	In other words, if removing ads led to 20% of smokers quitting, that would be a significant reduction in the tendency to smoke, even though 80% of smokers continued smoking, so as a group they were unlikely to quit once the ads stopped.
103	
104	More importantly, this answer only deals with existing smokers who may / may not quit. The other way that removing ads is thought to reduce smoking is by reducing the number of first-time smokers. This answer doesn't address that group at all.
105	
106	If removing tobacco ads causes some smokers to quit (even if a minority of them) but also causes many people to never take up smoking in the first place, then the author would still be right to say that removing these ads had a significant causal impact on the tendency to smoke.
107	(B) This argument isn't ever differentiating between broadcast media and print media.
108	
109	It's talking about nations that impose stringent restrictions on [all kinds] of tobacco advertising.
110	
111	Whether broadcast is more / less / equally stringent compared to print media doesn't affect the argument at all.
112	(C) Like most correct answers on Weaken when we're doing an Explain Curious Fact argument, this weakens by suggesting an Alternative Explanation for the curious fact.
113	
114	The author looked at the fact that nations that impose stringent restrictions on tobacco ads have significant reductions in the number of people smoking and assumed that restricting the ads caused the reduction in smoking.
115	
116	But whenever we have a correlation between X and Y, and then the author assumes that X causes Y, we immediately consider two alternate possibilities:
117	- Reverse Cause (maybe Y causes X?)
118	- 3rd Factor (maybe Z causes X and Y?)
119	
120	This is a 3rd Factor type of answer.
121	
122	"Increasingly widespread negative attitudes toward tobacco use" are leading to there being restrictions on tobacco ads and are leading to there being reductions in smoking.
123	
124	By analogy, suppose we said, "Research shows significant reductions in the number of people who have sex before marriage in those families who go to church every week. Thus, going to church every week as a significant causal impact on the tendency towards pre-marital sex."
125	
126	The 3rd factor alternate explanation would be, "No, no --- being very religious is the causal factor that explains why they're going to church every week and why they're 'saving themselves' for marriage."
127	(D) This answer has nothing to do with hurting the plausibility that "tobacco ads have a significant causal impact on the tendency to smoke".
128	
129	And it's not offering an alternate explanation for why countries with stringent restrictions on tobacco ads have significant reductions in the number of people smoking.
130	
131	Thus, it's useless to us. It's just a random fact about the sticking power of smoking, if you start in your adolescence.
132	(E) Do we even care about "other kinds of advertising"? Probably now. However, we could hurt the plausibility that "tobacco ads have a significant causal impact on the tendency to smoke" by pointing to other forms of advertising and saying that they have little causal impact on people's tendency to do what's being advertised.
133	
134	For example, an answer like "advertisements for casino gambling / alcohol have little causal impact on the tendency to gamble or drink alcohol" would weaken somewhat.
135	
136	But this isn't saying that other types of advertisements tend to have little causal impact. It's saying, for the group of people that are not impacted by tobacco advertising, they are also not impacted by other types of advertising.
137	
138	It might be that 95% of people are impacted by tobacco advertising and other types. This answer is only talking about those who aren't, but it isn't giving us any sense of whether that's normal or abnormal when it comes to advertising's impact on behavior.
139

143	STEM: Which one of the following would, if true, most undermine the argument?
144	(A) [1% | No Impact: largest populations] The countries with the largest populations do not have severe penalties for driving while intoxicated.
145	(B) [85% | Correct] Very severe penalties against driving while intoxicated are in effect only in countries in which alcohol use is rare.
146	(C) [1% | Unclear Impact: speed limit] The higher a country’s speed limits, the more frequent traffic accidents tend to be in that country.
147	(D) [10% | Weak Impact] Only a relatively small minority of those who drive while intoxicated are actually apprehended while doing so.
148	(E) [3% | No Impact] All countries impose severer penalties on those who cause accidents while driving intoxicated than on those who are merely apprehended while driving intoxicated.
149	--- BREAKDOWN
150	## Conclusion
151	This refutes those who claim that would-be drunk drivers will not be deterred by the prospect of severe penalties.
152	  rephrase: Would-be drunk drivers will be deterred by the prospect of severe penalties.
153	## Evidence
154	In countries with the most severe penalties for drunk driving, a smaller percentage of drivers have traffic accidents involving alcohol.
155	## Evaluate
156	Any time the evidence includes a statistic, study, artifact, or experiment, it's likely that the author's conclusion will announce or assume some causal interpretation of that evidence. We use our Explain Curious Fact mindset for these.
157	  Curious Fact .... why is there a correlation between countries with severe penalties for drunk driving and lower % of drivers having traffic accidents involving alcohol?
158	  Author's Explanation ... the harsh penalties scare citizens out of drunk driving.
159	  As always, we consider the two pressure points of such an argument:
160	  1. Alternate Explanations for the curious fact
161	  2. the plausibility of the author's explanation
162	  Whenever the author presents a correlation between X and Y and concludes/assumes that X causes Y, we reflexively consider two possible alternate explanations:
163	  - Reverse Cause (maybe Y causes X)
164	  - 3rd Factor (maybe X and Y are both connected to some other factor Z)
165	  Here, it doesn't make much sense to say that "Having a lower percentage of drivers involved in alcohol related accidents led to having more severe penalties for drunk driving". So, if we're going to weaken via Alternate Explanation, it will probably be a 3rd factor. For example, maybe the countries with the most severe penalties for drunk driving also happen to have cities that are more walkable / public transportation that is more usable.
166	  We could say, "the low % of alcohol accidents isn't because of the severe penalties. It's because people can walk or take the bus/train, rather than drive home."
167	## Goal
168	It's more common to weaken with an Alternate Explanation, so we'll primarily look for that, but we might otherwise see a correct answer that hurts the plausibility of the idea that "severe penalties are discouraging people from otherwise drunk driving" (f.e. maybe citizens have no idea what the penalties are for drunk driving).
169	--- CHOICE NOTES
170	(A) The raw number of population size makes no difference in this argument, because the statistic we're examining is talking about the percentage of drivers involved in alcohol related accidents.
171	
172	These countries with unusually low %'s of drivers involved in alcohol related accidents could be among the most populous countries, the least populous, or both.
173	(B) This identifies a 3rd Factor Alternate Explanation.
174	
175	The author thinks that alcohol related traffic accidents are low because the severe penalties act as a deterrent to keep people from drunk driving.
176	
177	This answer allows us to say, "Nah, the reason you see less alcohol-related accidents in this country isn't because the drunk driving laws are severe; it's just because alcohol use is very rare."
178	
179	There might be countries where the dominant religion heavily discourages drinking. And the laws of that country put very severe penalties against drunk driving, but really what's keeping there from being alcohol-related accidents is that people there don't drink (for religious reasons).
180	
181	This answer is technically a conditional. If we think it would be useful, we would think to ourselves about how the contrapositive sounds:
182	
183	```
184	If alcohol use in a       →    then there are not severe
185	country is common              penalties for drunk driving
186	```
187	
188	This makes it seem like the effect of "severe penalties" on drunk driving is never really getting tested. We wouldn't test the effectiveness of a mosquito repellant in an area where mosquitoes were rare.
189	
190	If there were some rule like, "Wherever mosquitoes are common, you won't find this mosquito repellant", then we really have no way to judge whether the repellant actually discourages mosquitoes.
191	(C) This answer is impossible to relate to the conversation at hand, because it talks about speed limits and traffic accidents (in general, not specifically alcohol-related ones).
192	
193	We'd have no way to connect this information to the topic at hand.

200	If, however, those drunk people knew that only a small minority of drunk drivers are ever caught in the act, they would have less to fear and thus be less deterred from drunk driving.
201	
202	However, this answer isn't clarifying whether drunk people who are considering drunk driving are actually aware that only a small minority of drunk drivers get caught. They might not realize they have a good chance of not being apprehended.
203	
204	Furthermore, "only a relatively small minority" could still be like 15% of drunk drivers. If I told you, "there's only a 1 in 6 chance you'll get caught if you drive home drunk, but if you do, you know that the severe penalty is 20 years in jail", would you be deterred? Or would you accept those odds?

213	STIMULUS: Economist: Gifts of cash or gift cards, which allow the recipient to choose the actual gift, are more highly valued by recipients than are gifts chosen for them by others. In a study, when people were asked how much they would have been willing to pay for gifts chosen for them by others, they responded by citing amounts that were on average only about two-thirds of the actual price of the gifts.
214	STEM: Which one of the following, if true, most seriously weakens the economist’s argument?
215	(A) [1% | Strengthens] The rate at which gifts are returned to retailers has been steadily increasing since the rate was first measured.
216	(B) [5% | Irrelevant] Gifts of cash and gift cards currently represent only about 14 percent of all gift giving.
217	(C) [34% | Unclear Impact] People in the study would have been willing to pay more for gifts chosen for them by close friends and relatives than for gifts chosen for them by others.
218	(D) [58% | Correct] People are unwilling to sell gifts chosen for them by others unless offered about one and a half times the gift’s actual price.
219	(E) [1% | Irrelevant: receipts] Most retailers require receipts before people can return gifts for refund or exchange.
220	--- BREAKDOWN
221	## Conclusion
222	Cash / gift cards are more highly valued by recipients than are gifts chosen for them by others.
223	## Evidence
224	In a study, people were asked how much they'd pay for a series of gifts that people had bought them, and the average price they said was only about 2/3 the cash price of the gift.
225	## Evaluate
226	Given That
227	  people only said they'd spend 2/3 what their gifts were worth
228	  How Can We Argue That
229	  people value gifts given to them by others more than they would the cash equivalent?
230	  We could say that monetary value is only part of "value". What about sentimental value? My daughter might only pay $10 for the $15 penguin stuffed animal that I got her when I was in Patagonia, but that doesn't mean that the penguin I gave my daughter is only worth $10 to her.
231	  As a commodity on the shelf, she thinks it's worth about $10. As a totem of my love for her, she wants to keep it forever.
232	  Similarly, we often buy gifts for people that are more indulgent than what they would spend on themselves. I will only pay about $35 for a bottle of scotch, as will many of my scotch-drinking friends. But if I'm buying a gift for one of them, I'll buy the $50 bottle for them.
233	  Would they pay $50 for that bottle of scotch? No. But would they rather have $50 or the bottle of scotch?
234	  Possibly the bottle of scotch. If they just had the $50, they would spend it frugally. That's not as much fun as someone else spending $50 indulgently in some "Treat yo'self" type way.
235	## Goal
236	Look for an answer that talks about the more complex ways we evaluate the value of gifts (more than just what we would pay for it). Maybe an answer will provide a story where we would see more value in the overpriced but thoughtful gesture than we would in an equivalent cash gift.
237	--- CHOICE NOTES
238	(A) This reinforces the idea that people aren't buying us great gifts (we're returning the gifts more and more), and so it argues in favor of just giving us cash or a gift card instead so that we can' choose our own gift.
239	(B) It doesn't make any difference what % of gifts are currently cash/gift cards vs. items.
240	
241	If I'm trying to argue that "solar panels are more valuable than wind turbines", it doesn't affect my argument one way or the other which one is currently more prevalent.
242	(C) At first this seems kind of tempting. Granted, the conclusion is just comparing "cash/gift cards vs. things chosen by others". The "others" isn't segregated, so do we really care about gifts from friends/relatives vs. gifts from other people?
243	
244	No, but we kind of like this answer because it seems like the "what about sentimental value" objection we were thinking about.
245	
246	The problem is that this answer choice doesn't control for the fact that friends/relatives are usually going to spend more on our gift than other people would. I might buy a $10 candle for someone I don't really know, whereas I'll buy that $50 bottle of scotch for my brother.

248	The fact that the person I gave the candle to would only pay about $6.70 for it, while my brother would only pay about $35 for the scotch might be all that this answer choice is telling us.

250	In both cases, the author's argument still holds. Since the recipient was only willing to pay 2/3 of what I spent, then wouldn't they have rather just had the cash I spent?
251	
252	If this answer had controlled for the price of the gift, it would have been better:
253	"People in the study would have been willing to pay more for the same gift if it had been chosen by a friend/relative than if had been chosen by others"
254

257	But even then it would be a weak objection, because we'd be qualifying the conclusion, "In cases where the gift comes from a friend/relative, we value the gift more than the equivalent cash value."

259	The conclusion is averaging all gifts together in a big lump sum comparison.

285	She provided a Correlation, which is an LSAT author's beloved launchpad for a causal conclusion. The most common form of correlation language sounds something like:
286	people who are X are more likely than those who aren't to be Y
287	The final claim is basically saying "People being treated for these three conditions are more likely to have lower Mg in their blood".
288	Does the author think that being treated for these conditions causes you to have lower Mg in your blood? Or does the author think that having lower Mg in your blood causes these conditions?
289	She evidently thinks the latter, because she's envisioning this causal chain:

291	We can use our Explain Curious Fact mindset, since the author is assuming a causal interpretation of a curious fact.
292	Curious Fact.. how come the people treated for these conditions have lower Mg in their blood? Author's Assumed Explanation.. lower Mg in your blood increases your risk of these three conditions.
293	We know we're supposed to consider 1. could there be an Alternate Explanation for that curious fact? 2. how plausible is the Author's Explanation?
294	If an author presents a correlation between X and Y and assumes that X causes Y, the two alternate explanations we want to always consider are: - REVERSE CAUSE (maybe Y causes X) - 3RD FACTOR (maybe both X and Y are attached to some third factor Z, which is the real causal factor)
295	## Goal
296	On Weaken, when we're dealing with an Explain Curious Fact type reasoning move, the most common form of answer is to provide an Alternate Explanation for the curious fact.
297	So we would primarily look for a different way to explain why "lower Mg" and "these three conditions" are correlated.
298	But we can also consider any answer that would help us argue that drinking mostly soft water **doesn't** lead to a higher risk of those three conditions.

499	STIMULUS: Most respondents to a magazine survey who had recently listened to a taped reading of a certain best-selling novel said that they had enjoyed the novel, while most respondents who had recently read the novel themselves said they had not enjoyed it. These survey results support the contention that a person who listens to a taped reading of a novel is more likely to enjoy the novel than a person who reads it is.
500	STEM: Which one of the following, if true, would most weaken the argument?
501	(A) [8% | No Impact] Most of the respondents who had listened to a taped reading of the novel had never read it, and most of the respondents who had read the novel had never listened to a taped reading of it.
502	(B) [2% | No Impact] Most people can read a novel in considerably less time than it would take them to listen to a taped reading of it.
503	(C) [7% | No Distinction] When people are asked their opinion of a best-selling novel that they have read or listened to on tape, they are more likely to say that they enjoyed the novel than that they did not enjoy it.
504	(D) [2% | Irrelevant Distinction: available] Many novels that are available in text versions are not available in audio versions.
505	(E) [81% | Correct] The novel in question, unlike most novels, included dialogue in many different dialects that are more understandable when heard than when read.
506	--- BREAKDOWN
507	## Conclusion (these results support)
508	A person who listens to a taped reading of a novel is more likely to enjoy the novel than a person who reads it is.
509	## Evidence
510	In a survey, most respondents who had recently listened to a taped reading of a certain best-selling novel said they enjoyed the novel, while most who read the novel said they didn't enjoy it.
511	## Evaluate
512	Since the conclusion is offering an explanation for the curious asymmetric comparison in the survey results, we can use our Explain Curious Fact mindset. Curious Fact ... why is it that most who listened to the novel said they enjoyed it while most who read the novel said they didn't enjoy it? Author's Explanation ... people are more likely to enjoy hearing a taped version of a novel than reading the novel itself. We want to consider Alternate Explanations as well as the plausibility of the Author's Explanation. Maybe a different explanation for the survey results is simply that the particular person who did the voiceover really added a lot to the experience (in a way that isn't typical for novels). Maybe most novels are read by their authors, but this one was read by someone with an iconic voice or great speaking/acting talents. Maybe the sort of person who listens to a novel is different from the sort who reads one. Perhaps people who listen to novels are less discerning about the quality of what they're hearing so they are more likely to rank it favorably, whereas people who read novels are literature snobs that insist on high quality. To test the plausibility of the author's theory, we'd want to test the same reader with both the taped / written version of the novel and see if they prefer the taped version to the written version. Or we'd want to try another 10 novels with the same respondents and see if we keep getting results where the listeners had more favorable reactions than the readers.
513	## Goal
514	Since we're trying to Weaken, we want to either offer an Alternate Explanation for why the listeners liked the novel better than the readers did, or to hurt the plausibility of the conclusion by making this novel seem atypical (since the conclusion is making a generalization about all taped vs. written versions of novels).
515	--- CHOICE NOTES
516	(A) Although we could strengthen this argument by saying, "People experienced both the taped version and the written version and almost always preferred the taped version", it doesn't weaken the argument to learn that most people did not experience both versions.
517	This answer can't be said to much impact, since we basically already knew this to be true. The background facts of the survey made it clear that most people did not experience both versions, since most who listened said they enjoyed the novel and most who read said they didn't enjoy it.
518	If most respondents had listened to it and read it, then we would be able to say "most of the listeners said the novel was good" and "most of the readers said the novel was bad" because most of the listeners were also readers (and vice versa).
519	(B) This presents a distinction - reading takes less time than listening. Could that be the real reason why the listeners liked the novel more than the readers?
520	For us to think of that as an Explanation, we'd have to assume that "the less time it takes to experience a novel, the less likely you are to enjoy it". That's not really a common sense link (for myself, the opposite probably applies).

It's just saying the probability is higher for positive reviews than for negative ones.

529	(D) It doesn't matter whether audio versions always exist. The author is promising that we can find an audio version of every book.

538	STIMULUS: Company president: Most of our best sales representatives came to the job with a degree in engineering but little or no sales experience. Thus, when we hire sales representatives, we should favor applicants who have engineering degrees but little or no sales experience over applicants with extensive sales experience but no engineering degrees.
539	STEM: Which one of the following, if true, most seriously weakens the company president's argument?
540	(A) [17% | No Impact] Some of the company's sales representatives completed a degree in engineering while working for the company.
541	(B) [46% | Correct] Most of the people hired by the company as sales representatives have had a degree in engineering but no sales experience.
542	(C) [4% | Strengthens, if Anything] Most of the customers that the company's sales representatives work with have a degree in engineering.
543	(D) [11% | Out of Scope: % of applicants] Most of the people who apply for a sales representative position with the company do not have a degree in engineering.
544	(E) [23% | Too Weak] Some of the people who the company has hired as sales representatives and who were subsequently not very good at the job did not have extensive previous sales experience.
545	--- BREAKDOWN
546	## Conclusion
547	When we hire sales reps, we should favor applicants who have engineering degrees but little sales experience over applicants with lots of sales experience but no engineering degree.
548	## Evidence
549	Most of our best sales reps came to the job with engineering degrees but little sales experience.
550	## Evaluate
551	The author is taking a trait possessed by most of her best sales reps and assuming that this trait is causally important to their superlative performance at this job. She could have just as easily argued, "Most of our best sales reps were born in Pennsylvania, so when we're hiring sales reps, we should favor applicants who were born in Pennsylvania". This is a Correlation → Causality type move. Curious Fact: Why is it that most of our best sales reps had engineering backgrounds, but no sales experience? Author's Story: Apparently this type of background makes you a great salesperson at this company. We can weaken this by appealing to one of two pressure points: 1. what is some OTHER WAY to explain why most of the best sales reps have this engineering + no sales background? 2. how could we make the AUTHOR's WAY seem less plausible, i.e. how can we make it seem like this engineering / no sales background is not one of the main reasons for their success as sales reps?
552	## Goal
553	Whenever we're weakening a Causal interpretation, we either want an Alternate Explanation for the curious fact or to hurt the Author's Explanation.
554	--- CHOICE NOTES
555	(A) First of all, "some" is so weak that it is almost always wrong for Strengthen, Weaken, and Paradox questions (which answer, if true, most ____ ).
556	Secondly, the evidence is about these super awesome sales reps who came to the job with a degree in engineering. The fact that other employees got a degree in engineering while working for the company doesn't tell us anything. Are those people also good sales reps? Did their abilities as sales reps get better after they'd completed their engineering education? Worse / same?
557	(B) This provides an Alternate Explanation for the curious fact.
558	Why is it that most of our best sales reps have an engineering / no sales background?
559	It's not because that type of background makes them more likely to succeed; it's just because most of the employees have that background.
560	If most people are right handed, then most criminals should be right handed. That doesn't mean that being right handed makes you more likely to be criminal. It's just the idea that if we don't think two things are related, then their proportion in the general population should match their proportion in the specific population.
561	If 90% of people are right handed, and if we don't think that there is any causal connection between being right vs. left handed and being a criminal, then we would expect 90% of criminals to be right handed.
562	When things are disproportional, then we know there is some weird causal factor at play. When people of color are only 40% of drug users but 65% of people arrested for drug use, then we start to think, "is race/ethnicity playing some causal role in whether or not you get arrested for drug use?"
563	So if 70% (i.e. "most") of the employees at this company were born in Pennsylvania, then you'd expect 70% of the best sales reps to be born in PA.
564	If 70% of the employees have an engineering + no sales background, then you'd expect 70% of the best sales rep to have that background.
565	Since we can explain the predominance of this background among the best sales reps as a simple fact of the underlying population of employees, we have less reason to believe the author's thinking, that this background is a secret causal-difference maker that makes someone a better sales rep (which she implicitly believes, if she thinks in future hiring we should be prioritizing this type of background).
566	(C) For the most part, the background of the customers is completely out of scope. But, in sales, relating to your customers can be a common sense reason for being a more effective salesperson.
567	So if most of the customers have engineering degrees, that actually helps boost the plausibility of the author's story, that this engineering background is actually helping the sales reps to do better, because we could say it's allowing them to better relate to the company's customers.
568	(D) The percent of applicants who have one background vs. another doesn't really help us here. We might think that this answer weakens by reducing the feasibility of the conclusion.
569	i.e. Good luck hiring more sales reps with engineering backgrounds .... most of your applicants don't have them.
570	However, that's a pretty weak objection. A company might get 50 or more applications for a job opening. Even if only 2 out those 50 have engineering backgrounds, the author's conclusion that we should favor those applicants is still viable.
571	(E) "Some" is so weak that it is almost always wrong for Strengthen, Weaken, and Paradox questions (which answer, if true, most ____ ). Some means "at least one", so there's usually not much impact by saying there's at least one data point with certain qualities.
572	This just says "at least one sales rep who lacked sales experience wasn't very good at the job".
573	This barely relates, since the demographic profile the author wants to favor is "engineering degree + little sales". Just saying "little sales experience" doesn't mean we're talking about the type of applicant the author wants to hire.
574	However, even if this answer said "some sales reps who had engineering degrees but not sales experience were not very good" would still not weaken much at all.
575	When our premise is a "most" or a "more likely to" or "on average", then the evidence happily tolerates exceptions. Saying that MOST of the best sales reps have engineering degree + no sales is not guaranteeing that anyone with that background will be great, so saying "there's at least one person with that background who's not great" doesn't go against anything the author is saying or thinking.

578	=================== PT121-S4-Q9 | diff 3 | key A | score 23.1 | features: Explain Curious Fact, Alternatives, Medical
579	STIMULUS: A recent study of several hundred female physicians showed that their tendency to develop coronary disease was inversely proportional to their dietary intake of two vitamins, folate and B6. The researchers concluded that folate and B6 inhibit the development of heart disease in women.
580	STEM: Which one of the following would, if true, most weaken the researchers' conclusion?
581	(A) [73% | Correct] The foods that contain significant amounts of the vitamins folate and B6 also contain significant amounts of nonvitamin nutrients that inhibit heart disease.
582	(B) [2% | Unclear Impact] It is very unlikely that a chemical compound would inhibit coronary disease in women but not in men.
583	(C) [2% | Irrelevant Comparison: nonphysicians / No Impact: know a great deal] Physicians are more likely than nonphysicians to know a great deal about the link between diet and health.
584	(D) [21% | Weaker Impact] The physicians in the study had not been screened in advance to ensure that none had preexisting heart conditions.
585	(E) [2% | Weaker Impact] The vitamins folate and B6 are present only in very small amounts in most foods.
586	--- BREAKDOWN
587	## Conclusion (concluded that)
588	Folate and B6 inhibit the development of heart disease in women.
589	## Evidence (study)
590	The more folate and B6 a person took, the less likely they were to develop coronary disease.
591	## Evaluate
592	Whenever we see that the evidence is a study or experiment, we brace ourselves for a causal conclusion. LSAT authors love to look at the results of a study or experiment and then conclude some causal explanation for the data coming out of it. Here, the causal verb in the conclusion is **inhibit**.
593	The data from the study is usually a Curious Fact (an asymmetric comparison like a correlation or statistical change or difference between two groups). In this case it was a correlation between higher intake of folate/B6 and lower risk of heart disease.
594	Here, the author is thinking, "Huh -- how come heart disease risk was inversely proportional to consumption of folate and B6? I know -- folate and B6 **cause** your heart disease risk to go down." Any time the test presents a correlation between X and Y, and then the author concludes that X causes Y, we are supposed to reflexively consider the two famous Alternate Explanations: - Reverse Cause (maybe Y caused X) - 3rd Factor (maybe something else associated with X is causing Y) Would it make any sense here to say that "having the lower risk of heart disease is actually causing increased intake of folate/B6"? No, that seems weird.
595	Is it possible that something else associated with high intake of folate/B6 is the real cause of the lower heart disease risk? Sure!
596	## Goal
597	We can weaken an Explain Curious Fact type argument one of two ways 1. suggest an Alternate Explanation for the curious fact 2. undermined the Plausibility of the Author's Explanation
598	So let's look (primarily) for an alternate way to explain the correlation between high intake of folate/B6 and low risk of heart disease.
599	Or we'll look (secondarily) for an answer that makes it less plausible that consuming folate/B6 lowers women's risk of heart disease.
600	--- CHOICE NOTES
601	(A) Here's our Alternate Explanation for why there's a correlation between high intake of folate/B6 and lower risk of heart disease:
602	
603	it's not because the folate/B6 is inhibiting heart disease; rather it's OTHER ingredients in the food that are inhibiting heart disease.
604	
605	Maybe bananas have a lot of folate and B6 in them -- the female doctors who eat lots of bananas are consuming lots of folate and B6, but it's really the **potassium** in bananas that is inhibiting the heart disease.
606	
607	This is the classic "3rd Factor" style of Alternate Explanation. Something associated with the supposed cause (folate/B6) is the actual cause.
608	(B) Folate and B6 are chemical compounds. If they have little to no effect on coronary disease in men, then according to this answer it would be very implausible that they would have any effect on coronary disease in women.
609	
610	So this would weaken, **if** we knew that these chemical compounds had little to no effect on coronary disease in men.
611	
612	We have no idea whether or not they do. The fact that the conclusion says "these two thing inhibit heart disease in women" doesn't imply that this only works for women.
613	
614	Since we don't know whether folate/B6 seem to have any heart disease effect on men, there's no way to know whether this answer strengthens or weakens.
615	(C) The study only dealt with physicians. **Among** physicians, there was a correlation between more intake of folate/B6 and less risk of heart disease.
616	
617	We would need an alternate explanation to explain why the physicians who consume more folate/B6 had lower risk of heart disease than the physicians who consumed less. It's hard to make nonphysicians relevant to this conversation, and we would only care about what they're actually eating, not about what they KNOW about the connection between diet and health.
618	
619	- paragraph
620	(D) This does sound like a methodological weakness of the study, but this wouldn't beat (A) in terms of impact.
621	
622	Since the study relates consumption of folate/B6 to the propensity **to develop** heart disease. If they turn out to already have heart disease, then they wouldn't be included in this correlation. Or, if the study is measuring folate/B6 intake over the course of a lifetime, then it's fine to have some data points where the physician already developed heart disease. You can still connect that to their folate/B6 intake prior to getting heart disease.
623	
624	It also doesn't matter for the study whether every participant is free of heart conditions (heart condition ≠ heart disease). There might be some participants with an irregular heartbeat, which would be a heart condition but not heart disease.
625	
626	So while this answer does make us a little nervous about how the data in the study was compiled, there's not a clear enough objection presented by this answer to outdo the objection we get from (A).
627	(E) This might seem to make the author's explanation feel a little less plausible: how could intake of folate/B6 make a difference if they're only present in very small amounts in most foods?
628	
629	But there are easy ways to respond to that: - regardless of the tiny amount, the physicians who had MORE folate/B6 had LESS risk of heart disease
630	
631	- the physicians who had more folate/B6 ate those atypical foods that are high in folate/B6
632	
633	- the physicians who had more folate/B6 intake got those vitamins via supplements, not foods

636	=================== PT111-S1-Q13 | diff 3 | key D | score 22.6 | features: Belief / Knowledge / Intent, Explain Curious Fact, Alternatives
637	STIMULUS: Journal: In several psychological studies, subjects were given statements to read that caused them to form new beliefs. Later, the subjects were told that the original statements were false. The studies report, however, that most subjects persevered in their newly acquired beliefs, even after being told that the original statements were false. This strongly suggests that humans continue to hold onto acquired beliefs even in the absence of any credible evidence to support them.
638	STEM: Which one of the following, if true, most undermines the journal’s argument?
639	(A) [18% | Weaker Impact] Regardless of the truth of what the subjects were later told, the beliefs based on the original statements were, for the most part, correct.
640	(B) [4% | Out of Scope: unrealistic] It is unrealistic to expect people to keep track of the original basis of their beliefs, and to revise a belief when its original basis is undercut.
641	(C) [3% | Strengthens, if anything] The statements originally given to the subjects would be highly misleading even if true.
642	(D) [72% | Correct] Most of the subjects had acquired confirmation of their newly acquired beliefs by the time they were told that the original statements were false.
643	(E) [3% | No Impact] Most of the subjects were initially skeptical of the statements originally given to them.
644	--- BREAKDOWN
645	## Conclusion (This strongly suggests)
646	Humans hold onto acquired beliefs even in the absence of any credible evidence to support them.
647	## Evidence
648	In a study, people read statements that caused them to form new beliefs. Later when they were told that the original statements were false, the subjects maintained those new beliefs.
649	## Evaluate
650	The author's conclusion is extremely broad, speaking for all of humankind, based only on a sample of people in a study (who may not be representative). So it's possible that a Weaken answer would attack this *Sampling* move, where the author reasons, "If it was true for *these* people, it must be true for people in general."
651	
652	But we can also attack the notion that the conclusion was even true for the people in the study. Were they examples of "humans who continued to hold onto acquired beliefs even in the absence of any credible evidence to support those beliefs"?
653	
654	If we want to fight that categorization, we either have to find a way to argue that the people in the study:
655	1. DIDN'T hold onto acquired beliefs, or
656	2. DID have credible evidence to support those beliefs (or both)
657	
658	We can't argue that first line of objection, because the evidence explicitly says that most of them persisted in their newly acquired beliefs.
659	
660	Okay, so is it possible that they *did* have credible evidence to support those beliefs? They probably didn't have credible evidence for those beliefs *prior* to the study, since we know it was reading the statements in the study that caused the people to form these new beliefs.
661	
662	But in between the time they're reading these statements and forming beliefs and the time, "Later," when they're told the statements were false, could they have gotten credible evidence to support the belief?
663	
664	Sure, I guess so. You could read a statement like, "Barack Obama plans to invest in a passenger spaceship to Mars" and then form the new belief, "Within the next 50 years, people will be able to take a passenger spaceship to Mars".
665	
666	Later, we tell you that Obama statement we provided you with was false, but when you got home that day, you Googled "passenger spaceship to Mars" and found out that Elon Musk was actually planning to do this. Your newly acquired belief has some credible evidence, in addition to the statement we provided.
667	
668	Thus, when we rescind the truth of those statements, it doesn't cause you to jettison that new belief you formed about "within 50 years, passenger travel to Mars". You are **not** an example of someone who is holding onto an acquired belief even though you have no credible evidence to support it.
669	## Goal
670	Look for an answer impugning the Sampling move, by saying that what happened in this study is not a generalizable "human" trait.
671	
672	Or look for an answer exploiting the wiggle room with "Later, ...". Someone might have found credible evidence to support their new belief in the intervening period. Just because the original reason for the belief turns out to be false, that doesn't mean the belief is false (that's the *Unproven vs. Proven False* distinction).
673	--- CHOICE NOTES
674	(A) This helps weaken somewhat, because if the people who persisted in believing the new beliefs are believing **mostly true** things, it feels like it could help us make the counterargument that these people **did** have credible reason to believe them.
675	
676	They weren't just "stuck" believing them, because of the psychological trick of the experiment.
677	
678	But this answer doesn't establish that the subjects knew these beliefs were mostly correct or that the subjects had any credible evidence. An answer that establishes the subjects persisted in holding onto the new beliefs because they were aware of credible evidence for them is a stronger counter.
679	(B) This answer sounds like it's trying to shield these research subjects from the author's critical glare.
680	
681	"Jeez, author --- give them a break, alright? Yes, they look stupid because they're still believing the things they came to believe based on your phony statements, but people can't be expected to remember the origin of their beliefs or to "update their beliefs" when the basis of a belief is undercut."
682	
683	No one is blaming these research subjects for their behavior. The author isn't concluding, "They **should** have stopped believing those newly acquired beliefs". The author is descriptively (neutrally) concluding, "They continued believing those new beliefs even though (I assume) they had no credible reason to."
684	(C) This has the effect of making the original statements sound like fishy statements. "Even if true" suggests that there's a good chance they're not true. And even if they technically qualify as true, they're misleading.
685	
686	If people are stubbornly hanging onto beliefs even when they're told that the "unlikely / misleading" statements on which they based those beliefs are false, that seems to help the author.
687	
688	We don't have to worry whether it actually strengthens. We know it doesn't weaken, since it doesn't give us a way to argue that these subjects actually did have credible evidence to support the beliefs they were retaining.
689	(D) This goes with our anticipated objection. By the time people were told that Obama isn't really planning to build passenger ships to Mars, they had found out that Elon Musk genuinely was.
690	
691	Thus, when the researchers said, "The original statements we told you were false", the subjects were like, "Oh, were those statements false? Hmm. Cute. Well, ... the beliefs I ended up with on the basis of your false statements happened to be true (I know this, because I've acquired confirmation of them since then). So, I'm sticking with my new beliefs, no thanks to you mischievous researchers."
692	(E) If we had no better answers, maybe we could say that initial skepticism might prompt these research subjects to do some independent research on their own, and that that's why they now retain their newly acquired beliefs. They **do** have credible evidence!
693	
694	But naturally this answer isn't as compelling as the correct one, which spells out the idea that the subjects have their own credible evidence.

697	=================== PT122-S2-Q8 | diff 1 | key D | score 22.5 | features: Assumes Similarity
698	STIMULUS: 1990 editorial: Local pay phone calls have cost a quarter apiece ever since the 1970s, when a soft drink from a vending machine cost about the same. The price of a soft drink has more than doubled since, so phone companies should be allowed to raise the price of pay phone calls too.
699	STEM: Which one of the following, if true, most weakens the editorial's argument?
700	(A) [3% | Unclear Impact] A pay phone typically cost less than a soft-drink machine in the 1970s.
701	(B) [3% | Strengthens] Due to inflation, the prices of most goods more than doubled between the 1970s and 1990.
702	(C) [2% | Strengthens, if Anything] Government regulation of phone call prices did not become more stringent between the 1970s and 1990.
703	(D) [90% | Correct] Between the 1970s and 1990 the cost of ingredients for soft drinks increased at a greater rate than the cost of telephone equipment.
704	(E) [2% | Strengthens] Technological advances made telephone equipment more sophisticated between the 1970s and 1990.
705	--- BREAKDOWN
706	## Conclusion (so)
707	Phone companies should be allowed to raise the price of pay phone calls too.
708	## Evidence
709	Local pay phone calls have cost a quarter apiece ever since the 1970s. Vending machine soft drinks cost a quarter in the 1970s, but now they cost more than fifty cents.
710	## Evaluate
711	This is an argument by analogy: - Things A and B have trait X in common. - Thing B has trait Y. - So, Thing A must also have trait Y.
712	Pay phone calls and vending machine sodas both cost 25 cents in 1970. Vending machine sodas now cost 50 cents. So, pay phone calls should also cost 50 cents.
713	We attack arguments by analogy by saying, "Even though they have that first thing in common, they don't have that second thing in common, because [_____]."
714	We try to argue that the two things aren't fair to compare when it comes to thing Y. There's some important difference. For example, a soft drink is a luxury item, but a pay phone (in 1990, before cell phones) can be a necessity! We can't price people out of making pay phone calls, whereas we aren't afraid to price some people out of buying vending machine sodas.
715	## Goal
716	Look for an answer that completes this sentence "Even though the cost of sodas have gone up, the price of pay phone calls shouldn't go up too, because.... "
717	--- CHOICE NOTES
718	(A) This answer is about the hardware. The actual wall-mounted payphone or the vending machine itself. This argument was about the goods, the service those machines provide.
719	
720	However, the argument is about setting pricing, which is based on revenue and expense. So maybe we could say that since the machine costs of providing pay phone calls was lower than the machine costs of providing sodas, the price of a pay phone call doesn't need to be as high as that of a soda.
721	
722	But is it still that way? This is about the 70s. We need to know whether it's still cheaper to host pay phone calls than to host vending machine sodas.
723	
724	It's a big stretch to argue, "Because the hardware for phone calls was usually cheaper than the hardware for sodas, back in the 70s, the price of a pay phone call shouldn't be raised here in 1990."
725	(B) If the prices of most goods doubled, then all the more reason to support the author's notion that we should have seen a doubling in the price of a pay phone call by now.
726	(C) Would it matter if government regulation of phone call prices **had** become more stringent between the 70s and 90s?
727	
728	One possible objection to this argument could be, "pay phone prices shouldn't be raised because it's a public good; people might need to use these in the case of emergencies, so the government forces phone companies to keep the price artificially low at 25 cents."
729	
730	This answer is ruling out the idea that pay phone calls are intentionally kept low by the government via regulation. It removes a potential obstacle to the author's idea to raise phone call prices, so it very weakly strengthens (like when you affirm a necessary assumption).
731	(D) We were looking for a salient difference between pay phone calls and sodas.
732	
733	"Even though the cost of sodas have gone up, the price of pay phone calls shouldn't go up too, because .... " in the case of soft drinks, the price hike was merited by the rising cost of ingredients for soft drinks. Meanwhile, in the case of pay phone calls, the cost of telephone equipment hasn't gone up that much.
734	
735	The price of soft drinks needed to go up in order to stay profitable, but the price of pay phone calls doesn't need to go up, since the costs of phone equipment have barely increased.
736	
737	This is a pretty sloppy answer since it still implies that the costs of telephone equipment **have** increased, which would allow someone to say "Well, damn, regardless of what soda is doing, you're entitled to raise your price when your costs go up, Pay Phone Companies!"
738	
739	But in the sense that this is the only answer that creates some friction by making the two things being compared seem disanalogous, it's still our best available answer.
740	(E) This shows a change between 1970 and 1990 that makes phone equipment seem like higher quality (which can mean a justifiable raising of the price) as well as possibly a rise in costs, since more sophisticated equipment is usually more expensive equipment.
741	
742	The fact that phones might cost more to provide and also offer a better experience both support the idea that their price should no longer match the 1970 price.

745	=================== PT146-S2-Q22 | diff 5 | key D | score 22.5 | features: Explain Curious Fact, Alternatives, Worst of the Worst
746	STIMULUS: In a recent study, one group of participants watched video recordings of themselves running on treadmills, and a second group watched recordings of other people running on treadmills. When contacted later, participants in the first group reported exercising, on average, 1 hour longer each day than did the other participants. This shows that watching a recording of yourself exercising can motivate you to exercise more.
747	STEM: Which one of the following, if true, most weakens the argument?
748	(A) [2% | Irrelevant Comparison: lifting vs. running] In another study, people who watched recordings of themselves lifting weights exercised for more time each day than did people who watched recordings of themselves running.
749	(B) [4% | Weakly Strengthens] Another study’s members exhibited an increased willingness to give to charity after hearing stories in which people with whom they identified did so.
750	(C) [42% | No Impact] Participants who were already highly motivated to exercise did not report exercising for any longer each day than they had before the study.
751	(D) [49% | Correct] In studies of identical twins, participants who observed their twin reading overreported by a significant amount how much time they themselves spent reading in the days that followed.
752	(E) [4% | Strengthens] A third group of participants who watched recordings of themselves sitting on couches afterwards reported being sedentary for more time each day than did the other participants.
753	--- BREAKDOWN
754	## Conclusion
755	Watching a recording of yourself exercising can motivate you to exercise more.
756	## Evidence
757	In a study, the people who watched a recording of themselves running on a treadmill reported exercising, on average, 1 more hour per day than the people who instead watched other people run on a treadmill.
758	## Evaluate
759	This is a typical Curious Fact / Explanation kind of setup.
760	  Curious Fact: How come the ones who saw themselves in the video report exercising more than 1 hr more than those who saw other people exercising?
761	  Author's Explanation: Watching yourself on video exercising motivates you to exercise more, so these people who watched themselves during the study had new motivation to exercise, increased their exercise regimen, and now average 1 hr more of exercise.
762	  You'll notice I'm filling in a lot of blanks for the Author. That's a good habit when we're trying to understand the Causal story that they're selling. We always react to Curious Fact arguments with the same two questions:
763	  How ELSE could we explain the Curious Fact?
764	  What could help us assess the plausibility of the Author's Story?
765	  Most correct answers that weaken a Causal argument provide a different way to explain the background data. Why else could the group that watched themselves be exercising 1 hour more? Maybe that group was just already exercising 1 hour more on average. The language of the argument doesn't say that they've increased their amount of exercise. Maybe that group did increase their exercise, but not as the result of this experiment. Maybe a disproportionate number of that group recently hired personal trainers, and that is the reason why they're exercising 1 hour more. Maybe they live in a climate that's more conducive to exercise.
766	  If we wanted to weaken the plausibility, we could say stuff like, "People are so mortified by how they look when exercising that it makes them want to exercise less" / "in another study, people who exercised in front of a mirror exercised less than people in front of a wall"
767	## Goal
768	Pick an answer that gives us a different way to explain the Curious "1 hr extra" Fact,
769	  or an answer that undermines the story that watching yourself exercise motivates you to exercise more.
770	--- CHOICE NOTES
771	(A) We care about the distinction between watching yourself exercise and not-watching yourself, but this answer would help us evaluate the difference between watching yourself lift vs. watching yourself run.

The conclusion is about businesses that increased their reliance on computer tech (this could be a small farm that was previously 5% reliant on computers and now is 10% reliant). 908	STIMULUS: At a large elementary school researchers studied a small group of children who successfully completed an experimental program in which they learned to play chess. 909	STEM: Which one of the following, if true, most tends to undermine the argument?
910	(A) [2% | No Impact] Some students who did not participate in the chess program had learned to play chess at home.
911	(B) [21% | No Impact] Those children who began the program but who did not successfully complete it had lower preprogram levels of achievement than did those who eventually did successfully complete the program.
912	(C) [55% | Correct] Many of the children who completed the program subsequently sought membership on a school chess team that required a high grade average for membership.
913	(D) [15% | No Impact] Some students who did not participate in the chess program participated instead in after-school study sessions that helped them reach much higher levels of achievement in the year after they attended the sessions.
914	(E) [7% | No Impact] At least some of the students who did not successfully complete the program were nevertheless more talented chess players than some of the students who did complete the program.
915	--- BREAKDOWN
916	## Conclusion (Thus)
917	It's likely that the reasoning power and spatial intuition exercised in chess-playing also contribute to achievement in many other areas of intellectual activity.
918	## Evidence
919	In the study of elementary school children who completed an experimental program in which they learn to play chess, most of the children who completed the program soon showed a significant increase in achievement levels in all their schoolwork.
920	## Evaluate
921	Like so many other causal arguments, this argument establishes a correlation in the evidence and then introduces a causal verb (or implicit causal structure) in the conclusion.
922	  - The evidence provides a before/after correlation: most of the students who completed the chess program soon showed a big uptick in achievement levels in all their schoolwork.
923	  - The conclusion introduces a causal verb: The reasoning/spatial sense of chess contributes to achievement in other intellectual areas.
924	  - Since the author is looking at the results of a study / looking at something that changed over time, and then concluding a potential explanation for that data, we can use our Explain Curious Fact mindset:
925	  - Curious Fact ... Why did most of the kids who went through the chess program have an uptick in schoolwork afterwards?
926	  - Author's Explanation ... Chess improved their reasoning power / spatial intuition, and those improved faculties allowed them to do better on their schoolwork.
927	## Goal
928	Since this is Weaken, we look for two different types of correct answers (with a strong bias towards the first type):
929	  1. Could there be some Alternate Explanation for the curious fact? What could be a different reason why the students who went through the chess program generally had an increase in school achievement afterwards?
930	  2. Could we undermine the plausibility of the Author's Explanation? What could make it seem like chess didn't really improve the kids' reasoning or spatial intuition, or make it seem like even if it did, those improved faculties wouldn't be likely to cause a boost in school achievement.
931	--- CHOICE NOTES
932	(A) Students who didn't participate in the program are almost completely irrelevant to this argument.
933	
934	The only way to make them relevant would be in some Effect w/o Cause way, where we impugn the idea that "chess caused these chess students to improve at school" by saying, "even the kids who weren't in the chess program improved at school to a similar degree".
935	(B) The kids who didn't complete the program are irrelevant. We wouldn't even be able to use them in the Effect w/o Cause style alluded to for choice (A).
936	
937	After all, if a child began but didn't complete the chess program, then should we count them as a child who did receive the putative boost in reasoning power and spatial intuition, count them as someone who didn't? 50% boost?
938	
939	It would be completely unclear how to judge them, because "began but did not complete" doesn't tell us whether the student made it through only 1% of the program or through 90% of it before bailing.
940	
941	Also, comparing preprogram levels of achievement between students is irrelevant. This argument is only interested in what caused their achievement to rise. We don't care whether their starting point was low / medium / high.
942	
943	We would only care about what percentage of achievement increase the chess players had vs. the non-chess players.
944	
945	Regardless of preprogram levels, if chess players raised their GPA by 40% while non-chess players raised their GPA by 15%, that still presents the same curious fact of why the chess players are improving more than the non-chess players are improving.
946	(C) As we implicitly expect on Weaken questions for an Explain Curious Fact argument, this correct answer provides an Alternate Explanation for the curious fact.
947	
948	This allows us to argue, "hey, author --- the reason their schoolwork improved after going through the chess program isn't because chess improved their brain's ability to do schoolwork.
