1089	STIMULUS: Professor: Economists argue that buying lottery tickets is an unwise use of resources, because the average payoff for the tickets sold in a lottery is much lower than the cost of a ticket. But this reasoning is faulty. The average amount paid out on individual insurance policies is much lower than the average cost of a policy, yet nobody would argue that purchasing insurance is an unwise use of resources.
1090	STEM: Which one of the following, if true, most weakens the professor's argument?
1091	(A) [3% | Strengthens, if Anything] Individuals spend, on average, much more on insurance than on lottery tickets.
1092	(B) [3% | Strengthens, if Anything] Insurance companies generally retain a higher proportion of total revenue than do organizations that sponsor lotteries.
1093	(C) [4% | Strengthens] Taking small financial risks can often greatly increase one's chances of obtaining much larger benefits.
1094	(D) [38% | Term Shift: grand prize] In general, the odds of winning the grand prize in a lottery are significantly lower than the odds of collecting a settlement from a typical insurance policy.
1095	(E) [52% | Correct] The protection against loss that insurance provides is more important to one's well-being than is the possibility of a windfall gain.
1096	--- BREAKDOWN
1097	## Conclusion
1098	It's faulty to reason that lotto tickets are a poor use of money, just because the average payout for a ticket in a lottery is much lower than the cost of a ticket.
1099	## Evidence
1100	Nobody would say that individual insurance policies are a poor use of money, and they also have an average payout that's less than the amount paid by the policy.
1101	## Evaluate
1102	The easy part of this argument is that it's making a Comparison between lotto tickets and insurance policies, so if we're going to Weaken this Comparison, we're probably going to want an answer that points out an important difference between the two things. The hard part of this argument is understanding the whole "average payout" concept. We can remind ourselves that running a lotto and running an insurance company are both profitable ventures. They take in more money than they give out. Lotto's might collect 10 million in revenue and then pay out 1 million as the grand prize. Insurance companies might collect 10 million in premiums and then pay out 1 million to people who file claims when their property is damaged. Since the average payout of the lotto or an insurance policy would be calculated by thinking of how much money is paid in vs. how much money ever goes back to the customers, any profitable lotto or insurance company will have a smaller amount going back to customers than is being taken in. Put another way, it might cost $5 to buy a lotto card, but the average return on buying one is $2. An insurance policy might cost $500 and the average payout to policy holders is $75.
1103	## Goal
1104	In both cases, we know that we're putting money into a system that in the long run is meant to collect more money than will be distributed. (gamblers in Vegas know that "the House always wins", meaning, in the long run the casinos must take in more money than they give away) So how can we fight this conclusion and argue that "Even though both lotto tickets and insurance policies usually involve losing money, insurance policies are a wise use of resources and lotto tickets are an unwise use of resources?"

1109	This answer is only saying that one has a bigger pay-in than the other (but if it also has a bigger average pay-out, it could still be the wiser investment).
1110	(B) This is essentially saying that insurance companies have a higher profit margin than do lotto sponsors. In other words, with insurance companies, they're keeping more of the money and expending less on payouts to their customers.
1111	That would make them a bigger losing bet than lotto tickets, so that goes the opposite of our desired direction: "insurance is fine, lotto is dumb".
1112	(C) "Small financial risks for the chance of obtaining much larger benefits" sounds a lot like lotto tickets. This answer is saying something good about lotto tickets? No thanks.
1113	(D) This is definitely the most tempting answer, because it feels like it points to a salient difference that makes lotto tickets sound worse: you're way less likely to get a payout! But as written, this just establishes that you're less like to win the biggest prize than to get an insurance settlement. It's still possible, in this case, that the odds of winning something meaningful are as good as the odds of getting some insurance settlement, so it doesn't have a particularly strong impact on the argument.

Perhaps we will soon face climatic feedback loops from climate change that will evaporate tons of our water or taint our water or make a lot of it inaccessible. Maybe the (natural or man-made) distribution networks that move water from one place to another will get impaired.

1276	(D) [4% | Strengthens, if anything] People who are well informed about the risks posed by natural disasters tend to have become well informed in some way other than by watching television.

1279	## Conclusion
1280	Watching too much TV can lead people to overestimate the risks that the world poses.
1281	## Evidence
1282	Recent study showed a correlation (people who are X are more likely to be Y) between "thinking you'll be victims of a natural disaster" and "watching an above average amount of TV".
1283	## Evaluate
1284	Whenever we see correlation language or whenever we feel the author is interpreting the results of a study, we can use our Curious Fact / Explanation template.
1285	  - Curious Fact: Why is there a correlation between watching lots of TV and fearing you'll be victims of a natural disaster?
1286	  - Author's Story: Watching lots of TV leads you to overestimate the risks the world poses (so you over-panic about your likelihood of being killed in a natural disaster).
1287	  - The two pressure points for these arguments:
1288	    - how ELSE could we explain that curious fact?
1289	    - how plausible is the author's explanation?
1290	  - Since this is Weaken, we want to either
1291	    1. Suggest an alternate explanation for why there's a correlation between "lots of TV" and "fear natural disasters"
1292	    or
1293	    2. Hurt the plausibility of the storyline that watching too much TV causes you to overestimate the risks the world poses.
1294	  - A #1 style answer might give us the classic Alternate Explanation for Correlation forms such as
1295	    - Reverse Causality - People who know that natural disasters are coming to their area tend to watch more TV, to get updates about what to do, how to prepare, when to evacuate.
1296	    - Third Factor - People who live in Los Angeles are both threatened by earthquakes and wildfires (explaining the fear of natural disaster) and working in the entertainment industry (explaining the high level of TV watching).

1338	## Evidence
1339	There was a correlation from 1980 and 1986 between an uptick in the % of 18-year-olds recruited by the armed services and an uptick in the % of young people who dropped out of high school.
1340	
1341	18-year-olds are generally either high school graduates or high school dropouts.
1342	## Evaluate
1343	Since the evidence is presenting a *changing statistic*, and the conclusion is assuming a causal explanation for that statistic, we can use our *Explain Curious Fact* mindset:
1344	
1345	*Curious Fact ...* why was there an uptick in the % of 18-year-olds recruited into the armed services between 1980-1986? (why was this correlated with an uptick in % of 18-year-olds that were dropouts?)
1346	
1347	*Author's Explanation*.... the armed forces try hard to recruit high school dropouts, so during this period when there were more high school dropouts, they were able to recruit more 18-year-olds into the armed services.
1348	
1349	We can Weaken an *Explain Curious Fact* argument two ways:
1350	1. (more common) suggest an **Alternate Explanation** for the curious fact
1351	2. hurt the **plausibility** of the **Author's Explanation**
1352	
1353	Any time an author presents a correlation between X and Y and then assumes/concludes that X causes Y, we're supposed to reflexively consider these two *Alternate Explanations*:
1354	
1355	- could it be **Reverse Cause** (in this case -- could the rise in recruits have come first, and that somehow inspired more 18-year-olds to drop out?)
1356	
1357	- could there be some **Third Factor** responsible for both? (maybe a foreign invading army is causing people to drop out of school and sign up for the military)

We only know that the computer won the false negative battle. The doctor was more often guilty of a false negative, where she said it wasn't a heart attack but later it was confirmed that it was. But it could be that the computer was way worse at false positives. Maybe the computer almost always said "heart attack", so of course it nailed all the cases of genuine heart attacks but made tons of mistakes when the case was not actually a heart attack.

1615	STIMULUS: Politician: A major social problem is children hurting other children. The results of a recent experiment by psychologists establish that watching violent films is at least partly responsible for this aggressive behavior. The psychologists conducted an experiment in which one group of children watched a film of people punching Bobo the Clown dolls. A second group of children was not shown the film. Afterward, both groups of children played together in a room containing a Bobo doll. Most of the children who had seen the film punched the Bobo doll, while most of the other children did not.
1616	STEM: Which one of the following, if true, most weakens the politician's argument?
1617	(A) [3% | Too Weak] Some of the children who did not punch the Bobo doll, including some who had been shown the film, chastised those who did punch the doll.
1618	(B) [12% | Too Weak] The child who punched the Bobo doll the hardest and the most frequently had not been shown the film.
1619	(C) [73% | Correct] The children who had been shown the film were found to be no more likely than the children who had not been shown the film to punch other children.
1620	(D) [11% | Strengthens, if anything] Some children who had not been shown the film imitated the behavior of those who had been shown the film and who punched the doll.
1621	(E) [1% | No Impact] Many of the children who participated in the experiment had never seen a Bobo doll before the experiment.
1622	--- BREAKDOWN
1623	## Conclusion
1624	Watching violent films is at least partly responsible for children hurting other children.
1625	## Evidence
1626	In a study, the kids who watched a video of children hitting Bobo were more likely to subsequently hit Bobo than were the kids who didn't see that video.
1627	## Evaluate
1628	Since the author is interpreting the results of a study, we can use our Curious Fact / Explanation template. Curious Fact Why are the kids who watched the Bobo video more likely to hit Bobo than the kids who didn't see the video? Author's Story Watching violence on TV makes kids more aggressive. We always then consider two different pressure points: - How ELSE could we explain the curious fact? - how plausible is the author's explanation? Maybe the video-watching kids are hitting Bobo not because they learned violence from TV but because they just have cooped up energy, from having to sit still while watching the video. Maybe after any video, a kid has so much pent up energy that they're liable to act more aggressively. Maybe all kids feel the aggressive urge to hit the Bobo doll, but only the kids who watched the video realize that they're allowed to.
1629	## Goal
1630	Find an answer offering an alternate explanation for why the video-watchers were more likely to hit Bobo, an answer lowering the plausibility that watching "violent" TV causes more aggressive behavior, or an answer that allows us to accept the evidence but argue the anti-conclusion that "watching violent films is not a reason why children are hurting other children".
1631	--- CHOICE NOTES
1632	(A) Causal arguments almost always tolerate some exceptions. When you claim that "smoking cigarettes increases one's risk of lung cancer", it doesn't weaken your claim to say that "some people smoke cigarettes but don't get lung cancer".
1633	These are messy, real-world claims about tendencies, not absolutes.
1634	Our author is only claiming that watching the Bobo video made the video-watching children, on average, more violent around the Bobo doll than the other children were.
1635	The fact that some kids who didn't see the video still hit Bobo or that some kids who saw the video didn't hit Bobo (and chastised those who did) does not weaken this claim.
1636	(B) This is one paltry data point. The kid who hit Bobo the hardest didn't watch the doll. Cool. That doesn't make us re-imagine the data set we got — on average, the kids who watched the video were more likely to hit the doll.
1637	The single kid who struck it with the most force is not helping us to evaluate a large-scale trend, whether he watched the video or didn't watch the video.
1638	When we're doing Weaken with causal arguments, there is usually a trap answer saying, "there is at least one data point that doesn't fit the trend you described", and those have no impact.
1639	If I say that people who have Instagram accounts are more likely to take a picture every day with their phone, that isn't me promising that "every single person" with an IG account takes a pic per day on their phone. It's an average. It tolerates exceptions.
1640	(C) This basically establishes a big disconnect between the evidence and the conclusion.
1641	The author may have shown that "kids who watch a video about how other children behave around a toy are more likely to copy that behavior", but she doesn't get to use this Bobo study to say that "watching violent films makes kids more likely to hurt other children", if we're told that the video-watchers were no more likely than the other kids to punch other children.
1642	(D) This seems like if anything it would just support the idea that children copy what they see, which can help the author argue that if they watch violent films, they'll copy that violence.
1643	(E) We weren't really assuming that any of the kids had seen a Bobo doll before the experiment. In fact, the author presumably wants these kids to be blank slates, so that she can show how watching the video (or not watching it) "instructed" the kids how to behave around a Bobo doll.

1647	=================== PT153-S3-Q17 | diff 3 | key A | score 21.3 | features: Explain Curious Fact, Alternatives, Science
1648	STIMULUS: After monitoring blood levels of lycopene (a nutrient found in some fruits and vegetables) in 1,000 middle-aged study participants over a 12-year period, researchers found that participants with low levels of lycopene were more than twice as likely as those with high levels to have a stroke during that period. Clearly, lycopene reduces the risk of stroke.
1649	STEM: Which one of the following, if true, most weakens the argument?
1650	(A) [79% | Correct] Most fruits and vegetables that are rich in lycopene also contain high levels of several other nutrients that are thought likely to reduce the risk of stroke.
1651	(B) [2% | Strengthens] Countries in which people consume substantial quantities of lycopene-rich fruits and vegetables generally have lower rates of stroke than other countries.
1652	(C) [5% | Unclear Impact] Middle-aged people typically have lower lycopene levels than young adults.
1653	(D) [8% | No Impact] Study participants with high levels of lycopene consumed, on average, twice the quantity of fruits and vegetables as those with low levels of lycopene.
1654	(E) [5% | No Impact] There was wide variation in lycopene levels among study participants.
1655	--- BREAKDOWN
1656	## Conclusion (clearly)
1657	Lycopene reduces the risk of stroke.
1658	## Evidence
1659	In a study of 1,000 middle-aged people over a 12-year period, the people with low levels of lycopene were more than twice as likely as those with high levels to have a stroke during that period.
1660	## Evaluate
1661	We've got the classic pattern of "Evidence = Correlation" and "Conclusion = Causality". The most common formulation for "X and Y are correlated" is to say "People who are X are more likely than those who aren't to be Y". And then the conclusion is using the causal verb reduces. Whenever the author's conclusion assumes to states a causal explanation for the curious comparison presented in the evidence, then we go into our Explain Curious Fact mindset. Curious Fact ... why is low lycopene correlated with more strokes / why is high lycopene correlated with fewer strokes? Author's Explanation ... Lycopene reduces the risk of stroke. We always evaluate this template with the same two questions: 1. Can we think of an Alternate Explanation for the Curious Fact? 2. How plausible is the Author's Explanation? Any time we're given a correlation between x and y, and then the author assumes or concludes that x causes y, we automatically react with these two alternate explanation possibilities: - could it be y causes x? (Reverse Causality) - could it be z causes x and y? (3rd factor) Could having a stroke cause low levels of lycopene? Sounds weird, but maybe it's possible. Could there be some 3rd factor that both explains why someone would have low lycopene and explain why they have more strokes than average? Sure, maybe there's a genetic condition that leads to both. Maybe the people who consume the least lycopene have some other behavior that increases their risk of stroke (perhaps bodybuilders avoid fruits and veggies in favor of protein, so they have low lycopene, and the strain that weightlifting puts on their bodies increases their risk of strokes. That would create a correlation between low lycopene and more strokes, even though the lycopene wasn't causing the strokes.)
1662	## Goal
1663	Since this is Weaken, we primarily expect an answer to - suggest an Alternate Explanation for why low lycopene is correlated with strokes but we'd also consider an answer that - hurts the plausibility of the Author's notion that lycopene reduces the risk of stroke. That parenthetical detail that lycopene is "a nutrient found in some fruits and veggies" feels like a Breadcrumb, like a detail that didn't need to be in the paragraph, so it's probably in there because the correct answer is going to use it in some way.
1664	--- CHOICE NOTES
1665	(A) Here we get a 3rd Factor Alternate Explanation.
1666	
1667	The real causal factors are nutrients XYZ. They reduce the risk of stroke. They happen to be found in fruits and veggies that are rich in lycopene. So people who eat these fruits and veggies are getting their risk of stroke reduced by nutrients XYZ. And they're simultaneously ingesting a lot of lycopene.
1668	
1669	This explains why there ends up being a correlation between lycopene and fewer strokes, but lycopene isn't the causal factor reducing the risk of stroke; nutrients XYZ are.
1670	(B) This provides more data points that fit the author's hypothesis (More Cause, More Effect). She thinks that consuming lycopene causes lower risk of stroke, and this is talking about countries that eat a lot of lycopene and have a lower risk of stroke.
1671	(C) Because the answer fails to tell us whether middle-aged people have more / fewer strokes than young adults, we can't really do anything with it.
1672	
1673	If we made a common sense guess that "the older you are, the higher your risk of stroke", then this would strengthen, because the older middle-aged people have lower lycopene and more strokes, just like the correlation in the argument.
1674	
1675	Just like (B), this would represent more data points that support the plausibility of the author's conclusion.
1676	(D) This is just explaining how they got higher lycopene, and it's probably what we were already assuming.
1677	
1678	If I tell you that lycopene is found in fruits and veggies and that Group A has more lycopene in their system than Group B does, then you'll probably think, "I guess Group A ate more fruits and veggies." This answer doesn't have anything to do with assessing the connection between lycopene and stroke or with a potential alternate explanation for why lycopene and stroke risk would be correlated.
1679	(E) This is the classic wishy-washy wrong answer we see on Strengthen and Weaken:
1680	- things change
1681	- levels fluctuate
1682	- differences exist
1683	
1684	It tells us nothing we weren't already assuming. The results of the study are averaging together everyone's different lycopene levels and stroke risks and finding a strong correlation between the two. It doesn't matter that there was a wide variety of data points that went into that average.

1688	STIMULUS: Animal feed should not include genetically modified plants. 1691	(B) [2% | No Impact] The rats tended to eat more of the genetically modified potatoes at the beginning of the 30 days than they did toward the end of the 30 days.
1692	(C) [15% | No Impact: deformities at birth] Intestinal deformities at birth are not uncommon among rats bred in laboratory conditions.
1693	(D) [8% | No Impact: same nutritional value] Genetically modified potatoes have the same nutritional value to rats as do potatoes that are not genetically modified.
1694	(E) [13% | Too Weak] The researchers conducting the study were unable to explain how the genetic modifications of the potatoes would have caused the intestinal deformities or a weakened immune system in the rats.

1696	## Conclusion
1697	Animal feed shouldn't include any GMO plants.
1698	## Evidence
1699	In a study, rats that were fed GMO potatoes for 30 days tended to develop intestinal deformities and a weakened immune system, whereas rats fed a normal diet of non-GMO food didn't have these problem.
1700	## Evaluate
1701	Since the author is making a cause/effect assumption about something that went down with a study, we can use our Curious Fact / Explanation mindset. Curious Fact How come the rats fed GMO potatoes ended up with intestinal deformities and a bad immune system, whereas rats fet a non-GMO diet didn't have those problems? Author's Explanation The GMO potatoes caused the gut and immune system problems. We always affect these arguments in one of two ways: - Alternate Explanations for the curious fact or - Plausibility of the Author's Explanation What else could explain why the GMO potato group ended up with gut / immune problems? Well ... potentially anything. Were we ever told that the two groups were otherwise fair to compare? Were they kept in the same environment? Were they allowed to sleep the same amount? Were they given similar exercise? Were there any other potentially funky parts of the GMO potato rats' diet? Since the argument failed to establish that the only difference between these two groups was GMO potatoes vs. non-GMO potatoes, there is a whole world of potential alternate causes for why those rats got gut / immune problems (that would have nothing to do with the GMO-aspect of the potatoes they ate).

1703	Since this is Weaken, we would want a correct answer to suggests a different explanation for why the rats in the GMO potato group ended up with gut / immune problems, or we would want a correct answer to say something that undermines the plausibility that GMO potatoes were really to blame. Or, we would like any answer that goes against the conclusion and gives us a way to argue that animal feed should have GMO plants in it.

1711	(B) This answer doesn't quite add or subtract plausibility to the GMO-potato hypothesis. Since we know the GMO potato-eaters ended up with gut problems, it makes sense that they would be eating less of everything as the study went on.

1782	=================== PT153-S2-Q13 | diff 2 | key B | score 20.9 | features: Analogies, Assumes Similarity
1783	STIMULUS: The use of ordinary dictionaries in interpreting the law is justified in the same way that chemists use the periodic table. The periodic table is a convenient source of agreed-upon background information that can be usefully applied to the problem on which a chemist is working. In the same way, ordinary dictionaries can be useful to a legal interpreter in resolving terminological issues.
1784	STEM: Which one of the following, if true, most seriously weakens the argument?
1785	(A) [4% | Weak Impact] The periodic table lists the properties of the elements, and presents them in a pattern to represent relations between them, while an ordinary dictionary mostly just gives an alphabetical ordering to the words it defines.
1786	(B) [88% | Correct] There is wide agreement about the data on the periodic table, while disagreements between the definitions in different ordinary dictionaries are likely to be relevant to legal interpretation.
1787	(C) [0% | No Impact] The use of a periodic table as a reference source actually came much later in history than the use of ordinary dictionaries to describe the meanings of words.
1788	(D) [1% | No Impact] The periodic table contains only a relatively small amount of information that could, in theory, be memorized, while the information in an ordinary dictionary is likely to be too large for any one person to know all at once.
1789	(E) [6% | No Impact] The periodic table is used primarily by chemists, while ordinary dictionaries are not used primarily by legal scholars and legal interpreters.
1790	--- BREAKDOWN
1791	## Conclusion (justified = opinion)
1792	Using ordinary dictionaries to interpret the law is justified, just like a chemist using the periodic table.
1793	## Evidence
1794	The periodic table is a convenient source of agreed-upon background info that a chemist can use on the problem on which she's working. Similarly, an ordinary dictionary can be used by a legal interpreter to resolve terminological issues.
1795	## Evaluate
1796	This argument relies on an Analogy, signified by "in the same way". The author is trying to present chemists' using the periodic table as analogous to a lawyer or other legal interpreter's using a dictionary. Whenever we're trying to weaken an argument based on an analogy, our primary thought is, "How could these two different situations be meaningfully different? What makes them not fair to compare?" The periodic table is a tool used specifically by chemists. It contains information, such as atomic mass and number of electrons, that chemists use in their professional field. Meanwhile, a dictionary is not a tool specifically for lawyers. So is it just as useful as a professional tool? Are ordinary definitions of words the same as legal definitions of words? I'm guessing that if I looked up the word corporation in the dictionary, I wouldn't see any part of the definition refer to corporations "as people", yet in American law (thanks to Citizens v. United), the legal definition of corporation involves treating it like a person.
1797	## Goal
1798	So let's look for something pointing out a salient difference between using an ordinary dictionary to interpret law and using a periodic table to do chemistry. We want an answer that helps us argue that it wouldn't be a good idea for a law professional to use an ordinary dictionary to interpret law.
1799	--- CHOICE NOTES

183	=================== PT139-S1-Q5 | diff 3 | key C | score 21.7 | features: Explain Curious Fact
184	STIMULUS: Journalist: A recent study showed that people who drink three cups of decaffeinated coffee per day are twice as likely to develop arthritis—inflammation of joints resulting from damage to connective tissue—as those who drink three cups of regular coffee per day. Clearly, decaffeinated coffee must contain something that damages connective tissue and that is not present in regular coffee.
185	STEM: Which one of the following would be most useful to know in order to evaluate the journalist's argument?
186	(A) [16% | Unclear Impact] whether people who exercise regularly are more likely to drink decaffeinated beverages than those who do not
187	(B) [4% | Irrelevant Comparison] whether people who drink decaffeinated coffee tend to drink coffee less often than those who drink regular coffee
188	(C) [72% | Correct] whether the degeneration of connective tissue is slowed by consumption of caffeine and other stimulants
189	(D) [1% | Irrelevant] whether most coffee drinkers drink more than three cups of coffee per day
190	(E) [6% | Irrelevant Comparison] whether people who have arthritis are less likely than the general population to drink coffee of any kind
191	--- BREAKDOWN
192	## Conclusion (clearly)
193	Decaffeinated coffee must contain something that isn't present in regular coffee that damages connective tissue.
194	## Evidence
195	In a study, people who drink 3 cups of decaf every day were twice as likely to develop arthritis as people who drink 3 cups of regular coffee per day.
196	## Evaluate
197	Any time the author's conclusion is stating or assuming a causal explanation for the results of a study, we go into Explain Curious Fact mode. Curious Fact ... How come people who drink 3 cups of decaf are twice as likely to get arthritis, compared to people who drink 3 cups of regular? Author's Explanation ... There is a special ingredient in decaf, not in regular, that damages connective tissue, which leads to the arthritic inflammation of joints. We're always supposed to consider 1. Alternate Explanations for the curious fact 2. the plausibility of the Author's Explanation Any time we're given a correlation between x and y (in this case between decaf and arthritis), there are two automatic alternate explanations we want to consider: - could it be Reverse Causality? is it possible that the arthritis came first, and since the arthritis made it harder to grasp the coffee mug securely, those people switched to decaf so they wouldn't have the shaky hands a caffeinated person might have? The problem with that storyline here is that the correlation seems to have a built in before/after. It says that the people drinking decaf are more likely to develop arthritis (in the future). If it said they were more likely to have arthritis, then we could definitely consider this reverse causality angle. - could there be a Third Factor that is the real causal agent, and the two parts of the correlation are just symptoms of that third factor? is it possible that "old age" is the common ingredient? If older people are more likely to drink decaf and more likely to develop arthritis, then that would explain why decaf and arthritis are correlated, but it wouldn't be decaf causing the arthritis. When it comes to the plausibility of the author's story, she has to assume that the process of decaffeinated coffee involves adding some new ingredient. If it's only removing caffeine, then decaf and regular coffee are chemically the same, other than caffeine, and so her story would make no sense.
198	## Goal
199	Let's look for answer that either allows us to argue that there's an Alternate Explanation for why decaf is correlated with higher rates of arthritis, or one that deals with the plausibility of the author's storyline that decaf has a special ingredient that damages connective tissue.
200	--- CHOICE NOTES
201	(A) This answer is tempting, because it's potentially identifying a Third Factor.
202	Exercising regularly is correlated with decaf, so could it be that exercising regularly is really what's causing the higher rates of arthritis?
203	This is definitely worth keeping on a first pass, but is there a good common sense connection between "regular exercise" and "developing arthritis"? Does regular exercise damage connective tissue?
204	It really depends on the exercise, which is why ultimately it's unclear what impact this has. Running or playing basketball on hard surfaces is probably damaging to joints, but swimming or yoga presumably is not (and may even strengthen connective tissue).
205	(B) The study controlled for how much coffee was consumed by the participants. Both the decaf drinkers and the regular drinkers were consuming 3 cups per day.
206	So we can't devise an alternate explanation for these study results that involves different amounts of consumption.
207	(C) This presents an Alternate Explanation for why the decaf drinkers were twice as likely as the regular coffee drinker to develop arthritis:
208	it's not (as the author is concluding) that decaf contains something bad that increases your likelihood of getting arthritis, it's that regular coffee contains something good (caffeine, which is not in decaf) that decreases your likelihood of getting arthritis.
209	This is a tricky alternate explanation, because the coffee is still the causal reason for the difference in arthritis risk, but saying that "caffeinated coffee has an ingredient that helps to discourage arthritis" is a different explanation than "decaf coffee has an ingredient that helps to encourage arthritis.
210	If you want to try a similar problem with that sort of trick, try this one.
211	(D) It doesn't matter whether at least 51% of coffee drinkers drink more than 3 cups a day, or whether it's only 49% at most that drink more than 3 cups a day.
212	Neither one of those has any effect on us judging whether or not decaf has an ingredient that damages connective tissue.
213	(E) The study controlled for how much coffee was consumed by the participants. Both the decaf drinkers and the regular drinkers were consuming 3 cups per day. So this answer can't possibly offer us an alternate explanation for the study results.
214	If an answer said, "Whether people who have arthritis are advised by their doctors to avoid decaf coffee" that would be somewhat relevant, because it would increase the plausibility that there is something chemically dangerous about decaf when it comes to arthritis.

247	--- CHOICE NOTES

252	Which dogs were the ones who weren't given a treat? Dogs who were accustomed to receiving regular rewards, the other dogs, a mix of both?

255	(B) If the answer is YES, then it badly weakens the argument. It wouldn't be plausible to blame the decline in obedience on "unfair treatment" if there was still a decline in obedience when neither dog got a treat.

259	If the answer is NO, then it strengthens the argument.

284	(E) We aren't going to learn anything about what caused the decline in obedience among the unrewarded dogs by learning the precise number of reps before they began to disobey.

403	STIMULUS: Businessperson: Because the parking area directly in front of the building was closed for maintenance today, I was late to my meeting. If the maintenance had been done on a different day, I would have gotten to the meeting on time. After finding out that I could not park in that area it took me 15 minutes to find an available parking space, making me a few minutes late.
404	STEM: The answer to which one of the following questions would be most useful to know in order to evaluate the reasoning in the businessperson’s argument?
405	(A) [2% | Out of Scope: reasons for maintenance] What were the reasons for performing maintenance on the parking area directly in front of the building on that particular day?
406	(B) [30% | Unclear Impact] Were any other of the meeting attendees also late to the meeting because they had difficulty finding parking?
407	(C) [51% | Correct] What are the parking patterns in the building’s vicinity on days when the parking area in front of the building is open?
408	(D) [16% | Weak Impact] Does the businessperson have a tendency to be late to meetings?
409	(E) [1% | Out of Scope: important] Was it particularly important that the businessperson not be late to this meeting?
410	--- BREAKDOWN
411	## Conclusion
412	If the maintenance had been done on a different day, I would have gotten to the meeting on time.
413	## Evidence
414	Because of the maintenance, I wasn't allowed to park in the parking area directly in front of the building. After finding out that I couldn't park there, it took me 15 minutes to find a spot, which made me a few minutes late.
415	## Evaluate
416	The author's conclusion is about a counterfactual ... if they hadn't been doing maintenance that day. So to argue with him, we have to enter that alternative universe and think, how could we argue that in a world where they weren't doing maintenance in front of the building, he still would have been late? We know that he got to the lot around 15 minutes before his meeting (because a 15 min delay in finding a spot made him only a few minutes late). We'd pretty much be trying to think of storylines where arriving to the parking area 15 mins before the meeting isn't long enough. Maybe he still would have needed to cruise around 15 mins to find a spot? Maybe he would get caught in conversation with people and walk into the building? Maybe he never really has access to the front lot (either he's not important enough at the company to have a premier spot or he gets there late enough that the front lot is usually already full).
417	## Goal
418	It's hard to know what to predict here. We just know that the author is Assuming a Difference. He's either assuming that if the maintenance hadn't been going on, he would have been able to find a spot in the front lot, or that it would have taken less than 15 minutes to drive around and find a spot.
419	--- CHOICE NOTES
420	(A) It's irrelevant to us why they were doing maintenance. We're only concerned about the hypothetical world in which there wasn't maintenance closing down the front lot.
421	(B) Whether there were or weren't other people who were late because of finding parking won't really tell us anything about the hypothetical world where maintenance wasn't happening. We don't know whether they got to the lot around the same time the author did. Essentially, this answer is tempting us to object to this author by saying, "you're lying. the parking situation didn't really make you late. None of the other attendees were late because of the meeting." But that wouldn't be an LSAT type of objection: you're lying about your premise. We want to accept that it took 15 mins to find parking once he found out that the front lot was unavailable, but still argue that on a different day, he still would have been late.
422	(C) This is such an obnoxiously vague answer. Pretty legendary in terms of being gross. But, it is the only answer talking about the alternate universe where they weren't doing maintenance, and the front lot was open. If the parking patterns on regular days are such that the author would have been likely to be able to get a spot in the front lot at that time of day, that strengthens the argument. If the parking patterns on regular days are such that the author still wouldn't have found a spot in the front lot and still would have needed to circle around the parking lot a bunch to find a spot, then that weakens the argument.
423	(D) If we say "yes" to this question, we're saying "this person tends to be late to meetings". Does that weaken the argument? Yeah, it does, somewhat. It's not the strongest objection, because maybe this meeting was important and the author was atypically cautious about getting to work earlier. In fact, we know that the author was at least 10 mins early in getting to the parking lot (because the 15 min parking hunt only caused him to be a few minutes late). So, whether or not he's usually late to meetings, we already know he wasn't late to this one, in terms of when he got to the parking lot. We don't want to compare this meeting to other times there have been meetings. We're trying to compare how the author's parking went for this meeting to how the author's parking would have gone for this meeting if the maintenance hadn't closed down the front lot.
424	(E) Whether or not the meeting was important won't help us in any way to judge how the author's parking situation on this day would have compared with that on a regular day.

501	## Evaluate

504	EXPLANATION: eating the garlic tablet caused the cholesterol and triglyceride levels to go down.

592	would most help in evaluating the argument?

594	(B) [0% | Irrelevant Comparison] What percentage of the retail transactions studied were cash transactions?

596	(D) [1% | Irrelevant Distinction / Irrelevant Comparison] Did the people who returned the extra dollar suspect that it was given to them intentionally?
597	(E) [0% | Irrelevant Comparison] Does increasing the extra change to five dollars have an effect on people’s behavior?
598	--- BREAKDOWN
599	## Evidence
600	Customers who paid in cash at stores and received change were given an extra dollar with their change.
601	  Few people who received an extra dollar returned it.

603	Among those who received an extra dollar, most behaved dishonestly.
604	## Evaluate
605	This argument attempts to explain a curious fact: when people received an extra dollar, why did so few of them return it?
606	  By claiming that most of these people behaved dishonestly, the argument assumes that nothing else caused these people to keep the extra dollar. There are actually a number of reasonable explanations that have nothing to do with dishonesty. The argument never addresses any of these alternative explanations. Based just on the evidence we're given, there's no way we can be sure that any of these people behaved dishonestly.
607	  This type of question isn't extremely common in LR, so it's important to clearly understand what the question is asking and what to expect in the answer choices. The answer choices are all questions that can be answered with a short, factual answer. These will often be questions that can be answered with "yes" or "no."
608	  We need to think about a possible answer to each question, and consider what effect the answer would have on the argument. For example, if the answer to one question is "yes" that might strengthen the argument; however if the answer to that same question is "no" that might weaken the argument.
609	## Goal
610	One answer choice will pose a question that has a noticeable impact on the argument. The answer choice which poses that question will be the correct answer choice. The other questions will have much less impact, and possibly even no impact.

If a massively larger number of non-cash transactions were also studied, that has no impact on this argument. We only care about the cash transactions.

618	(C) The conclusion focuses on the people who did not return the extra dollar and does not rely on any comparison with the people who did return the extra dollar. It doesn't matter if people who returned the extra dollar describe themselves as honest or not.

621	(D) It can be easy to miss the fact that this answer is about people who did return the extra dollar. The LSAT might be trying to trick people who read this answer quickly.

685	STIMULUS: The ruins of the prehistoric Bolivian city of Tiwanaku feature green andacite stones weighing up to 40 tons. These stones were quarried at Copacabana, which is across a lake and about 90 kilometers away. Archaeologists hypothesize that the stones were brought to Tiwanaku on reed boats. To show this was possible, experimenters transported a 9-ton stone from Copacabana to Tiwanaku using a reed boat built with locally available materials and techniques traditional to the area.
686	STEM: Which one of the following would be most useful to know in order to evaluate the support for the archaeologists’ hypothesis?
687	(A) [89% | Correct] whether the traditional techniques for building reed boats were in use at the time Tiwanaku was inhabited
688	(B) [6% | Irrelevant] whether green andacite stones quarried at the time Tiwanaku was inhabited were used at any sites near Copacabana
689	(C) [1% | Irrelevant] whether reed boats are commonly used today on the lake
690	(D) [3% | Irrelevant] whether the green andacite stones at Tiwanaku are the largest stones at the site
691	(E) [2% | Irrelevant] whether the reed boat built for the experimenters is durable enough to remain usable for several years
692	--- BREAKDOWN
693	## Hypothesis
694	These huge 40 ton stones were transported from Copacabana to Tiwanaku, 90 km away and across a lake, on reed boats.
695	## Evidence
696	We transported some 9-ton stones from C to T using a reed boat built with locally available materials and techniques traditional to the area.
697	## Evaluate
698	Since we're dealing with the word 'hypothesis', we can use our Curious Fact / Explanation mindset. CURIOUS FACT - how did these prehistoric people get 40 ton stones from C to T?! AUTHOR'S EXPLANATION - they used reed boats There are two different ways to affect this sort of argument: - Alternate Explanations for the curious fact. - the Plausibility of the Author's Explanation What could be a different way that they got the 40 ton stones that far? Maybe the stones float? Maybe they broke them apart into smaller chunks and glued them back together? How plausible is the hypothesis that they used reed boats? Well, I wouldn't usually think of reeds as being strong enough to hold 40 tons. We can see from the experiment that the reed boats the experimenters made can carry 9 ton stones, but that's only 20% as heavy. Could the reed boats really handle 40 tons? In general, we have to wonder if the "proof of concept" voyage that the experimenters took with their 9 ton stones was relevantly similar to the reed boat voyage that the prehistoric people would have taken. If there are significant differences about terrain / weather / locally available materials, that might break down the comparability of the analogy.
699	## Goal
700	Look for an answer that could be answered in a way that would sound like an Alternate Explanation for how the prehistoric people moved 40 ton stones 90km from C to T, or for an answer that could be answered in a way that would damage the plausibility of the Author's Explanation.
701	--- CHOICE NOTES
702	(A) If the traditional techniques the experimenters used to build their reed boats weren't in use at the time Tiwanaku would have been building their reed boats, then the evidence the experimenters have offered is total garbage.
703	They made a boat using techniques that didn't exist with the Tiwanaku and carried much lighter stones ... that does not show that it's possible the Tiwanaku carried 40 ton stones with their style of reed boats.
704	(B) Whether these big stones were also used near the quarry or not used near the quarry doesn't illuminate anything about whether the Tiwanaku were able to move 40 ton stones across a lake on reed boats.
705	(C) Whether reed boats are commonly used today or rarely / never used today doesn't illuminate anything about whether the prehistoric Tiwanaku people were able to move 40 ton stones across a lake on reed boats.
706	(D) Whether the 40 ton stones were the largest stones or the 2nd largest or 3rd largest doesn't tell us anything about whether the prehistoric Tiwanaku people were able to move 40 ton stones across a lake on reed boats.
707	(E) This is the most tempting trap answer, but whether the reed boats built by the experimenters are now usable for several years or whether they only last one year doesn't really affect the plausibility of the hypothesis.
708	There was nothing in the prehistoric storyline that required that the reed boats the Tiwanaku built would last for several years, only that they were capable of a one-time move of 40 ton stones across a lake.

Doesn't that mean how much it cost to produce in YOUR country, not the country where you sell it? So if we're saying that Country F is dumping shrimp, we would be saying that Country F is selling its shrimp in another country for less than it cost to produce the shrimp in Country F. If these phenomena were caused by volcanic ash in the atmosphere, then the ash sent into the atmosphere by Mount Etna’s eruption must have spread over great distances.
845	STEM: In evaluating the support given for the conclusion advanced in the last sentence of the passage, it would be most important to determine whether

847	(B) [10% | No Impact: duration of weird weather] the abnormal weather in China lasted for a full year or longer
848	(C) [7% | No Impact] temperatures in Sicily were abnormally cold after Mount Etna erupted
849	(D) [78% | Correct] there were any volcanic eruptions near China around the time of Mount Etna’s eruption
850	(E) [1% | No Impact: subsequent eruptions] subsequent eruptions of Mount Etna were as powerful as the one in 44 B.C.
851	--- BREAKDOWN
852	## Conclusion
853	If the summer frosts / dimmed sunlight in China that occurred in the year following the eruption of Mount Etna were caused by volcanic ash in the atmosphere, then Mt. Etna's erupted ash must have spread over great distances.
854	## Evidence
855	China is thousands of miles east of Sicily.
856	## Evaluate
857	This conclusion is a conditional claim, so if we want to argue with it, we have to remind ourselves what it means to argue with or Refute a Conditional.
858	Arguing with a conditional is saying, "It's possible the trigger is true, but the outcome is not".
859	So in this case, we want to think about how we might argue that 1. the summer frost and dim sun in China was caused by volcanic ash in the atmosphere but 2. the ash sent into the atmosphere by Mt. Etna did not spread over great distances.
860	In order to reconcile those two, we'd either need to argue that ash doesn't have to spread all the way from Italy over to China in order for a volcano in Italy to affect China's climate.
861	Or we could argue that there was volcanic ash in the atmosphere from a source besides Mt. Etna.

866	(B) Whether the abnormal weather in China lasted a full year or only 10 months doesn't help us ascertain whether or not the abnormal weather in China was caused by ash spreading over great distances from Italy.
867	(C) We fully expect Sicily to have been abnormally cold after the eruption, because it's right underneath the ash cloud of Mt. Etna, so much sunlight will be blocked.
868	
869	If temperatures weren't that cold after the eruption, that would seem to mean it wasn't that bad an eruption or that the ash from the eruption quickly dispersed from the area around Sicily, or that Sicily was going through an abnormal heat spell around the time of the eruption so the cooling from the eruption only brought the temperature down to a normal amount.
870	
871	It doesn't seem to tell us anything with any direct impact on the cold Chinese summer.
872	(D) This provides us with a potential way to weaken the conclusion.
873	
874	To weaken the conclusion we need to figure out how the trigger could be true while the outcome is false.
875	
876	How could it be that "the cold Chinese summer was caused by volcanic ash in the atmosphere" (it doesn't specify where the volcanic ash came from), but that the ash from Mt. Etna did not spread over great distances from Italy to China?
877	
878	We could say that the cold Chinese summer was caused by volcanic ash in the atmosphere from an eruption nearer to China!
879	(E) Whether other eruptions in the proceeding 2000+ years were as strong or weren't as strong does nothing for our efforts to solve the causal mystery of what was causing the cold Chinese summer of 43 B.C.

964	=================== PT145-S2-Q10 | diff 3 | key C | score 14.8 | features: Explain Curious Fact
965	STIMULUS: One year ago, a municipality banned dishwasher detergents containing phosphates. Anecdotal evidence indicates that many residents continued to use detergents containing phosphates; they just purchased them from out-of-town stores. However, it is clear that some residents did switch to phosphate-free detergents, since phosphate pollution from the municipal wastewater treatment plant decreased significantly in the past year.
966	STEM: The answer to which one of the following questions would most help in evaluating the argument above?
967	(A) [2% | No Impact] Why did many residents continue to use detergents containing phosphates?
968	(B) [2% | No Impact: phosphate-free] What pollutants, if any, are present in phosphate-free dishwashing detergents?
969	(C) [74% | Correct] Were any changes made in the past year to the way the municipality’s wastewater treatment plant treats phosphates?
970	(D) [21% | Irrelevant: where the water goes next] Does most of the phosphate pollution in the municipality’s waterways come from treated wastewater from the municipal treatment plant?
971	(E) [2% | Weak Impact] Did municipal officials try to stop people from bringing detergents containing phosphates into the municipality?
972	--- BREAKDOWN
973	## Conclusion (it is clear that)
974	Some residents did switch to phosphate-free detergents.
975	## Evidence (since)
976	Phosphate pollution from the municipal wastewater treatment plant decreased significantly in the past year.
977	## Evaluate
978	Since the author's conclusion is assuming a causal Explanation for a Curious Fact, we can use our Curious Fact / Explanation mindset. Curious Fact ... why did the phosphate pollution from the treatment plant decrease significantly over the past year? Author's Explanation ... some residents switched to phosphate-free detergents. We, of course, jump into action thinking - could there be some Alternate Explanation for the curious fact? - how plausible is the Author's Explanation?
979	## Goal
980	On Evaluate, we're looking for a question that could be answered in some way that would be an Objection. An Objection to this argument would either, suggest an Alternate Explanation for why municipal treatment plants have lower phosphate pollution this year, or undermine the Author's Explanation that some residents have switched to phosphate-free detergents.
981	--- CHOICE NOTES
982	(A) We don't care about people who are still using phosphate detergents. We're trying to ascertain whether any residents stopped using phosphate detergents. So interviewing the people who still use detergents won't help us at all.
983	(B) We're trying to ascertain what caused the treatment plant's phosphate pollution to go down. Phosphate-free detergents don't contribute to "phosphate pollution", so who cares what other sort of pollutants they may have? We are investigating whether anyone switched to phosphate-free detergents. Telling us the ingredients of those detergents doesn't help us figure out whether anyone switched to using them.
984	(C) This does what Weaken answers most typically do on Curious Fact / Explanation arguments -- it suggests an alternate explanation for the curious fact. Why did phosphate pollution from the treatment plant decrease this past year? The author is assuming it must be because some residents switched to phosphate-free detergent (so there was less phosphate going into the treatment plant). But she's failing to consider Alternate Explanations: what if the phosphate pollution decreased instead because they just installed better equipment or processes at the treatment plant?
985	(D) We're trying to figure out why less phosphate pollution came out of the plant last year. This answer is about where the water goes after it's come out of the plant, and whether it ends up being a significant source of something somewhere else. All that matters to this question is solving the causal mystery of why less phosphate pollution came out of the treatment plant. Is it because less phosphate entered the treatment plant? If so, why did less enter? Or is it because the treatment plant removed more phosphate while the wastewater was inside the plant? Once this water exits the plant and we measure the plant's output of phosphate as lower than before, we're done with caring about it.
986	(E) If officials did try to stop people from bringing detergents w/ phosphates into the municipality, then that would mildly strengthen the plausibility that some residents probably switched to phosphate-free detergents. But if we say, "No, officials didn't try to stop people from bringing illegal detergents into the city", it doesn't weaken. It's perfectly plausible that some residents might switch to phosphate-free detergents, given that one year ago the phosphate-variety was banned, with or without official trying to stop rulebreakers from crossing the border with their contraband detergent. The objection we get from (C) is that, "the treatment plant is emitting less phosphate because they improved their filtering processes this year, not because residents switched to phosphate-free." The objection we would get from (E) is that, "it's not plausible that some residents switched to phosphate-free detergents, because the city officials didn't try to stop people from bringing phosphate detergents into the city."

1005	  - Let's just get rid of four answers that "do the job" ... they raise some issue that could lead to us saying "It's not practical to get rid of nuclear and to rely instead on solar, wind, hydro, and sludge.
1006	--- CHOICE NOTES
1007	(A) If the current methods of disposing of sewage sludge by dumping do a lot of environmental damage, then taking that sludge and using it for deriving oil (rather than just dumping it somewhere) should be a net win for the environment.
1008	
1009	It sounds like we'd be "meeting our energy needs in a way that better protects the environment from harm than we do at present".
1010	(B) Here, whether we say the processes have been improved in recent decades or haven't (i.e. they're the same as they were 20-30 years go), it makes no difference.
1011	
1012	The process that takes sewage and separates it out into clean water and sewage sludge doesn't really have anything to do with the activist's hope. If it's been improved, that doesn't necessarily mean anything good for the environment. If it's the same as it's been for decades, that doesn't mean anything.
1013	
1014	The new thing being discussed is the idea of taking that sewage sludge, once it's been separated from the clean water in sewage, and then deriving an oil from it that can generate power. This answer has nothing to do with our new idea for what we do with sewage sludge. It's only talking about the process that leads to us getting sewage sludge in the first place.
1015	(C) It wouldn't be very practical to use sewage sludge as an energy source if the cost of producing and using oil from sewage sludge is not economically sustainable.
1016	(D) Ceasing nuclear power and starting to use sewage sludge power might not be a net gain for protecting the environment from harm if sewage sludge led to harmful global warming gases, whereas the nuclear power didn't.
1017	(E) Ceasing nuclear power and starting to use sewage sludge power might not be a net gain for protecting the environment from harm if sewage sludge led to waste products that are just as dangerous as those nuclear power created.
1018

1020	=================== PT152-S4-Q19 | diff 4 | key D | score 12.2 | features: Explain Curious Fact, Alternatives
1021	STIMULUS: European wood ants incorporate large quantities of solidified conifer resin into their nests. Conifer resin is a natural disinfectant that has been shown to kill strains of bacteria that can cause disease in wood ants. Thus, the wood ants’ use of conifer resin probably came about as a disease-protection measure.
1022	STEM: Which one of the following would be most useful to know in order to evaluate the strength of the argument?
1023	(A) [8% | Too Strong: very long] whether conifer resin retains its disinfectant properties over very long periods of time
1024	(B) [3% | Unclear Impact] whether the nests of European wood ants generally contain more conifer resin at some times of the year than at others
1025	(C) [8% | No Impact] whether any ant species other than European wood ants use conifer resin in their nests
1026	(D) [72% | Correct] whether the use of conifer resin affords structural benefits to European wood ants’ nests
1027	(E) [9% | No Impact] whether the disinfectant properties of conifer resin evolved as a disease-protection measure for conifer trees
1028	--- BREAKDOWN
1029	## Conclusion
1030	The wood ants' use of resin probably came about as a disease prevention measure.
1031	## Evidence
1032	The resin has been shown to kill strains of bacteria that can cause disease in wood ants.
1033	## Evaluate
1034	This has the feel of a Curious Fact / Explanation problem.
1035	  Curious Fact: Why do these ants put this resin in their nests?
1036	  Author's Explanation: They use it as a disease prevention measure, since it kills off bacteria harmful to them.
1037	  We always ask:
1038	  1. is there some ALTERNATE EXPLANATION for the curious fact?
1039	  2. how plausible is the AUTHOR'S EXPLANATION?
1040	  Could there be some other reason for the resin? Maybe it smells nice? Maybe it traps other bugs for food? Maybe it's the glue that holds together the nest?
1041	  How plausible is it that the ants use the resin to prevent disease? In places that don't have this bacteria, do they not use the resin? In places with lots of the bacteria, do they use more of it?
1042	## Goal
1043	Evaluate answer choices are questions or whether or not toss-ups. We want to find an answer where if we answered it in a certain way, it could be a good Objection to the argument.
1044	  So primarily I would be looking for an answer that hints at an Alternate Explanation for why they're putting resin into their nests.
1045	--- CHOICE NOTES
1046	(A) There's no way to answer this and get a big Objection. We could say, "I doubt they're using it as a disease-protection measure. After all ... resin doesn't retain its disinfectant properties for very long periods of time."
1047	
1048	The author would be like, "cool, but it retains its properties for a long time, and that's long enough. Also, they might continually replenish the supply of resin".
1049	(B) This fact could be useful if it were associated to the rise and fall of the strains of bacteria, but on its own it does nothing.
1050	
1051	A better version of this answer would sound like, "whether the nests generally contain more resin at times of the year when the bacteria is most prevalent".
1052	(C) Even if other ant species do also use resin in their nests, that would tell us nothing about whether or not they use it for disease-protection purposes, and that's what we're here to assess.
1053	(D) This presents an Alternate Explanation. We can object to the argument by saying, "The ants aren't using it as a disease-prevention measure. They're just using it because it's sticky and helps their nests to be more structurally stable".
1054	
1055	The most common way to weaken any Curious Fact / Explanation argument is with an Alternate Explanation.
1056	(E) If this were true, then it adds a little plausibility to the idea that resin has a disease-protection function for at least some species.
1057	
1058	But if resin didn't evolve as disease-protection for trees, it wouldn't in any way weaken the plausibility that ants are using the resin for that purpose.
1059	
1060	
1061	=================== PT143-S4-Q2 | diff 1 | key B | score 6.2 | features: Sampling, Math-y Stuff, Percentages, Rebuttals
1062	STIMULUS: Programmer: We computer programmers at Mytheco
1063	are demanding raises to make our average salary
1064	comparable with that of the technical writers
1065	here who receive, on average, 20 percent more
1066	in salary and benefits than we do. This pay
1067	difference is unfair and intolerable. Mytheco executive: But many of the technical writers
1068	have worked for Mytheco longer than have many
1069	of the programmers. Since salary and benefits
1070	at Mytheco are directly tied to seniority, the 20
1071	percent pay difference you mention is perfectly
1072	acceptable.
1073	STEM: Evaluating the adequacy of the Mytheco executive’s
1074	response requires a clarification of which one of the
1075	following?
1076	(A) [2% | Out of Scope] whether any of the technical writers at Mytheco once worked as programmers at the company
1077	(B) [94% | Correct] how the average seniority of programmers compares with the average seniority of technical writers
1078	(C) [4% | Out of Scope] whether the sorts of benefits an employee of Mytheco receives are tied to the salary of that employee
1079	(D) [0% | Out of Scope] whether the Mytheco executive was at one time a technical writer employed by Mytheco
1080	(E) [0% | Out of Scope] how the Mytheco executive’s salary compares with that of the programmers
1081	--- BREAKDOWN
1082	## Programmer's argument
1083	The 20% pay difference (writers earn more) is unfair and intolerable.
1084	## Executive's argument
1085	Salary is tied to seniority, and many writers have more seniority than many programmers, so the difference is acceptable.
1086	## Evaluate
1087	The executive's defense relies on writers being more senior on average than programmers. But "many writers have more seniority than many programmers" is weaker than it sounds — it's consistent with writers and programmers having similar average seniority, or even programmers being more senior on average. To know whether the seniority-based pay system actually justifies a 20% gap, we need to know how the average seniority of the two groups compares.
1088	## Goal
1089	Find the answer that targets this comparison: the average seniority of programmers versus the average seniority of technical writers.
1090	--- CHOICE NOTES
1091	(A) Whether some writers used to be programmers doesn't affect whether the seniority-based pay system actually justifies the 20% gap. Their job-history details would not change the average seniority comparison or the fairness analysis. Doesn't bear on the executive's defense.
1092	(B) This nails the gap. The executive defended the 20% gap by saying pay tracks seniority and "many writers" have more seniority than "many programmers." But that vague comparison is consistent with the two groups having similar average seniority — in which case the seniority story can't justify the gap. Knowing how the average seniority of programmers compares to the average seniority of writers tells us whether the seniority-based system actually predicts the 20% gap. If averages are roughly equal, the executive's defense fails.
1093	(C) The executive's defense already explicitly says salary AND benefits are tied to seniority. We don't need to clarify whether benefits are tied to salary as a separate matter. This doesn't bear on the seniority-comparison gap that actually drives the dispute.
1094	(D) The executive's personal job history is irrelevant to whether the seniority-based pay system justifies the 20% gap. The argument is about pay structure, not about the executive's biases or background.
1095	(E) The executive's salary isn't part of the comparison. The dispute is about programmers vs. writers; what an executive earns is a separate matter and doesn't affect whether seniority justifies the writer-programmer gap.
1096
